# Sharia

"Islamic law" redirects here. For other uses, see [Islamic law (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_law_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Saria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saria_\(disambiguation\)), [Shara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shara_\(disambiguation\)), [Shariyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shariyah_\(disambiguation\)), or [Shahriyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahriyar_\(disambiguation\)).

***Sharia*** ([/ʃəˈriːə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English); [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): ‏[شَرِيعَة](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D8%B4%D8%B1%D9%8A%D8%B9%D8%A9#Arabic)‎, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *šarīʿa*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'path \[to water]', IPA: [\[ʃaˈriːʕa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic)), also [transliterated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic) as ***Sharī'ah***, ***Shari'a***, ***Shariah***, or referred to as **Sharia Law** is a body of [religious law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_law) that form the [Islamic](6037917.md) tradition[^cite_note-Bassiouni-1][^cite_note-oxforddic-2][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlén2003chpt._2a-3] based on [scriptures of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_holy_books), particularly the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran)[^cite_note-4] and [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith).[^cite_note-Bassiouni-1] In Islamic terminology *sharīʿah* refers to immutable, intangible [divine law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_law), in contrast to *[fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh)* (Islamic jurisprudence), which refers to its interpretations by [Islamic scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama).[^cite_note-ODI-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] Sharia, or fiqh as traditionally known, has always been used alongside [customary law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urf) from the very beginning in [Islamic history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_history);[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9] it has been elaborated and developed over the centuries by [legal opinions](11259.md) issued by [qualified jurists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti) – reflecting the tendencies of [different schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Fiqh) – and integrated with various economic, penal and administrative laws issued by [Muslim](19541.md) rulers; and implemented for centuries by [judges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadi) in the courts[^cite_note-ODI-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] until recent times, when [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) was widely adopted in Islamic societies.

Traditional [theory of Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence) recognizes four sources for al-sharia: the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), *[sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah)* (or [authentic ahadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authentic_hadith)), *[ijma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)* (lit. consensus) (may be understood as *ijma al-ummah* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): إجماع الأمة) – a whole Islamic community consensus, or *ijma al-aimmah* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): إجماع الائـمـة) – a consensus by religious authorities),[^cite_note-Standke2008-10] and *[analogical reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas)*.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-Esposito,_John_2001-14] It distinguishes two principal branches of law, rituals (*[Ibadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadah)*) and social dealings (*[Muamalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muamalat)*); subsections [family law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_family_jurisprudence), relationships (commercial, [political / administrative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_aspects_of_Islam)) and [criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_jurisprudence), in a wide range of topics[^cite_note-ODI-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] assigning actions – capable of settling into different categories according to different understandings – to categories ([ahkam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam)) mainly as: [mandatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard), [recommended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustahabb), [neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubah), [abhorred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makruh), and [prohibited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram).[^cite_note-ODI-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] Beyond legal norms, Sharia also enters many areas that are considered private practices today, such as belief, worshipping, ethics,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoulsonEl_Shamsy2019-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010145-16] [clothing](68301.md), and lifestyle, and gives to [those in command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulu'l-amr) [duties to intervene and regulate them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enjoining_good_and_forbidding_wrong).

Over time with the necessities brought by sociological changes, on the basis of interpretative studies [legal schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhhab) have emerged, reflecting the preferences of particular societies and governments, as well as [Islamic scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama) or [imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_in_Shia_doctrine) on [theoretical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence) and [practical](11259.md) applications of laws and regulations. [Legal schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhhab) of [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) – [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi), [Maliki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maliki), [Shafiʽi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi%CA%BDi_school), [Hanbali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali), etc. – developed methodologies for deriving rulings from scriptural sources using a process known as *[ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)*,[^cite_note-ODI-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] a concept adopted by [Shiism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiism) in much later periods meaning mental effort.[^cite_note-Mohammad_Farzaneh_2015_6-17] Although Sharia is presented in addition to its other aspects by the contemporary [Islamist understanding](15012.md), [as a form of governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_aspects_of_Islam)[^cite_note-18] [some researchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revisionist_school_of_Islamic_studies) approach [traditional sīrah narratives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C4%ABrah) with skepticism, seeing the [early history of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) not as a period when Sharia was dominant, but a kind of "[secular Arabic expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabization)" and dating the formation of Islamic identity to a much later period.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

Approaches to Sharia in the 21st century vary widely, and the role and mutability of Sharia[^cite_note-21] in a changing world has become an increasingly debated topic in Islam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Beyond [sectarian differences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_schools_and_branches), [fundamentalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism) advocate the complete and uncompromising implementation of "exact/pure sharia" without modifications,[^cite_note-oxforddic-2][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmanat2009-22] while [modernists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Modernism) argue that it can/should be brought into line with [human rights](13831.md) and other contemporary issues such as democracy, [minority rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_rights), [freedom of thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_thought), [women's rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights), and [banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank) by new jurisprudences.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-naim96-24][^cite_note-hajjar2004-25][^cite_note-26] Several practices of Sharia have been deemed incompatible with [human rights](13831.md), [gender equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_equality), and [freedom of speech and expression](21401843.md).[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32] In Muslim majority countries, traditional laws have been widely used with[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200819-33] or changed by European models. Judicial procedures and legal education have been brought in line with European practice likewise.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] While the constitutions of most Muslim-majority states contain references to Sharia, its rules are largely retained only in family law[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] and [penalties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_jurisprudence) in some. The [Islamic revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival) of the late 20th century [brought calls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawah) by [Islamic movements](15012.md) for full implementation of Sharia, including *[hudud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud)* [corporal punishments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment), such as [stoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34]

## Terminology

&#x20;The [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) word ‏[شريعة](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D8%B4%D8%B1%D9%8A%D8%B9%D8%A9#Arabic)‎ (*šarīʿa*) derives from the root *š-r-ʕ*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007321-35] In the Quran, شريعة (*šarīʿa*) and its cognate ‏[شرعة](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D8%B4%D8%B1%D8%B9%D8%A9#Arabic)‎ (*širʿa*) occur once each, with the meaning "way" or "path".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007321-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] This term was borrowed into [Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish) as [شرع](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D8%B4%D8%B1%D8%B9#Ottoman_Turkish) (*şerʿ / şeriʿ*),[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41] which then entered the English language as ***Sheri***,[^cite_note-42] a term that was used in late 19th- and early 20th-century law-related works, along with the French variant *chéri*.[^cite_note-43]



[Islamic miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_miniature) of [Musa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_in_Islam) receiving the [Tawrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah_in_Islam) from the angel [Jibril](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel)

According to [lexicographical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicographical) studies, this word and its derivatives —in texts reflecting a rural or nomadic setting— refer to the watering of animals at a permanent water source or along the seashore. Another area of usage is associated with the concepts of extension or length and is non-religious in nature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007326-44] The word is cognate with the Hebrew *saraʿ* ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): שָׂרַע, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'lady') and is likely to be the origin of the meaning "way" or "path".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007326-44] Some scholars regard this as an archaic Arabic word bearing similarities to [Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha) (Jewish law)[^cite_note-haqq-45] and suggest that its adoption as a metaphor for a divinely ordained way of life stems from the importance of water in an arid desert environment..[^cite_note-weiss1998-46] The word شريعة (*šarīʿa*) was widely used by Arabic-speaking [Jews](25955086.md) during the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), being the most common translation for the word *Torah* in the 10th-century Arabic translation of the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) by [Saadia Gaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadia_Gaon).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007321-35] A similar use of the term can be found in Christian writers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007321-35] The Arabic expression *Sharīʿat Allāh* (شريعة الله 'God's Law') is a common translation of *Torat [Elokim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elokim)* (תורת אלוהים, 'Teaching \[of] God') and νόμος τοῦ θεοῦ ('God's Law' in Greek in the New Testament \[Rom. 7: 22]).[^cite_note-47]

### Contemporary usage

In everyday usage and the language of sermons, the terminological and semantic distinction of the word is disregarded; instead, it is used interchangeably with —and as a substitute for— the concept of [Dīn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C4%ABn), which encompasses areas such as belief, morality, and custom. For example, شريعة موسى (*šarīʿat mūsā*) means "law" or "religion of [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses)" and شريعتنا (*šarīʿatunā*) can mean "our religion" in reference to any monotheistic faith.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007321-35] Within [Islamic discourse](15012.md), شريعة (*šarīʿa*) refers to religious -strict- regulations governing the lives of Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007321-35] For many Muslims, the word means simply "justice," and they will consider any law that promotes justice and social welfare to conform to Sharia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] A related term *_al-qānūn al-islāmī_* (القانون الإسلامي, Islamic law), which was borrowed from European usage in the late 19th century, is used in the Muslim world to refer to a legal system in the context of a modern state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalderHooker2007323-48] [Mehmet Görmez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmet_G%C3%B6rmez) draws a distinction between the concepts of shar'i (derived from Sharia) and mashru' (lit. approved by Sharia, legal); he states that not every ruling based on the sources of Sharia (nass) can be accepted as mashru' —a term he interprets as implying conformity with the principle of justice.[^cite_note-49]

## Theories of origin and roots

Further information: [History of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Islam) and [Canonization of Islamic scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization_of_Islamic_scripture)

According to the traditionalist (*[Atharī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atharism)*) Islamic perspective, the fundamental [Principles of Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence) and Sharia are traced back to Muhammad[^cite_note-Forte-1978-8-50] through the Companions, who adopted his actions and approvals as a model ([Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah)) and transmitted this knowledge to subsequent generations in the form of [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] These narrations paved the way first for informal discussions and later for systematic legal thought; this framework was most authoritatively articulated during the eighth and ninth centuries by the great jurists [Abu Hanifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hanifa), [Malik ibn Anas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malik_ibn_Anas), [al-Shafi‘i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shafi%E2%80%98i), and [Ahmad ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Hanbal) —recognized as the founders of the [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi), [Maliki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maliki), [Shafi‘i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi%E2%80%98i), and [Hanbali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali) schools of [Sunni jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] Although modern historians initially accepted the general outlines of the traditionalist narrative, some have proposed new theories regarding the formation of 'fiqh',[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] because Sharia laws did not possess the shallowness or inadequacy that would stem from the daily life practices of a rural tribal society numbering only a few thousand people.[^cite_note-51]



[Safaitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safaitic) script with a figure of a camel on a red sandstone fragment, from [es-Safa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Safa_\(Syria\)), currently housed in the British Museum

&#x20;Research has shown that, as is the case with Islamic belief system, certain elements that can be considered precursors to Sharia law and rituals —spanning a wide spectrum from [acts of worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadah) to civil [mu'amalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'amalat) and [penal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_jurisprudence)— can be observed in neighboring Middle Eastern cultures and [pre-Islamic Arab religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia), some examples include the following; [Pilgrimage](23763.md) is mentioned in pre-Islamic [Safaitic-Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safaitic) inscriptions,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Jallad202241–44,_68-52] and continuity can be observed in certain details, especially in today's [hajj](23487999.md) and [umrah](1591421.md) rituals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDost2023-53] [The veiling order](68301.md), which distinguishes between [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_concubinage)[^cite_note-Fadl-54] and free [women in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam), also coincides with similar distinctions seen in pre-Islamic civilizations.[^cite_note-Ahmed_1992_15-55][^cite_note-El_Guindi-56] [Qisas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas) was a practice used as a resolution tool in inter-tribal conflicts in [pre-Islamic Arab society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia). The basis of this resolution was that a member from the tribe to which the murderer belonged was handed over to the victim's family for execution, equivalent to the social status of the murdered person.[^cite_note-57] The "condition of social equivalence" meant the execution of a member of the murderer's tribe who was equivalent to the murdered person. For example, only a slave could be killed for a slave, and a woman for a woman. In other cases, *compensatory payment ([Diya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_money_in_Islam))* could be paid to the family of the murdered. On top of this pre-Islamic understanding, a debate was added whether a Muslim can be executed for a non-Muslim during the Islamic period. The foundational verse for the Islamic rule of retaliation is [Al Baqara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Baqara) (2\:178): “O believers! Retaliation is prescribed for you in cases of murder: a free man for a free man, a slave for a slave, and a female for a female. But if the offender is pardoned by the victim’s guardian, then blood-money should be decided fairly and payment made courteously...”&#x20;

Cuneiform on the stele. Refer to adjacent text

In the [laws of Hammurabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_Hammurabi), [an eye for an eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eye_for_an_eye#In_Babylonian_law) was applied if a free man (avīlum) gouged out the eye/broke the bone of another free man of equal status; otherwise, a monetary penalty was paid.

[A novel and compelling perspective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Studies_\(book\)) —proposed by [Ignác Goldziher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ign%C3%A1c_Goldziher) in the late 19th century and elaborated upon by [Joseph Schacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Schacht)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] in the mid-20th century— sheds light on two key phenomenon in early Islamic history: the transformation of [urf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urf) (custom) —which originally held a primary role in lawmaking alongside caliphal decrees and [legal opinions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_opinion) (ra’y)[^cite_note-Jokisch-2018-393-58]— into [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) (a conceptual shift);[^cite_note-59] and the infiltration of [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith) into the later legal framework with a dominant role, despite their initially negligible role in legislation. This explanation, which refers to backward projection,[^cite_note-60] also contained clues —alongside others— as to how the hadiths could be so comprehensive. According to this view, the customs and traditions of populations in territories acquired through early conquests, as well as the legal and administrative legacies of the preceding Byzantine, Sasanian, Umayyad etc eras, were attributed to Muhammad via chains of transmission ([isnad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isnad)); this secured the social legitimacy required for the new legal paradigm, the theoretical foundations of which were established by al-Shafi‘i.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-63][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-70] In his view, the real architect of Islamic jurisprudence was [al-Shafi'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shafi'i), who formulated this idea (that legal norms must be formally grounded in scriptural sources) and other elements of classical legal theory in his work *[al-risala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Risala_\(al-Shafi'i_book\))*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJokisch2015-71]

Some historians have sought a middle ground:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJokisch2015-71] Early Islamic law emerged from a combination of administrative and community-based practices in [the conquered territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) and shaped by the religious and [ethical principles of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_ethics).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] While retaining certain aspects of pre-Islamic laws and customs —and modifying others— this legal system aimed to establish norms of Islamic conduct and resolving disputes arising within the community.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014125-74] This was a gradual process of development —taking place within the cultural and political context of the era and region— amidst retrospective constructs[^cite_note-75] and traditions that reflected the understandings and solutions of the social fabric; it unfolded in circles of learning where students and aspiring scholars gathered to acquire knowledge from a local master and deliberate on religious matters—circles that would later come to be known as schools of law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014125-74][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a31–35-76]

## Sociology

Further information: [Islamic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy), [Sociology of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociology_of_religion), and [Siyasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siyasa)

Further information: [Islamic ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_ethics), [Ḥiyal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E1%B8%A4iyal), and [Taqiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqiyya)

Old picture of Al-Farabi

Depiction of [al-Farabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Farabi), Islamic philosopher and political theorist

*[Fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh)* is traditionally divided into the fields of *[uṣūl al-fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence)* (lit. the roots of *fiqh*), which studies the theoretical principles of jurisprudence, and *furūʿ al-fiqh* (lit. the branches of *fiqh*), which is devoted to elaboration of rulings on the basis of these principles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneider2014-12] The initial confrontations with the normative, text- and tradition-centered approaches of Sharia proponents and the authority of the [ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama) began at an intellectual level with rationalists and philosophers over issues such as [the definition of good and evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enjoining_good_and_forbidding_wrong). While some jurists continued to impose strict rules on society —claiming they were "required by Sharia"— within the framework of their own methodologies, another group, unable to directly oppose Sharia or these rules, authored books on [Ḥiyal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E1%B8%A4iyal) (legal stratagems), that demonstrated Sharia-compliant ways to circumvent the established regulations. [Esoteric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batiniyya) and certain —marginal— [Sufi movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) viewed the exoteric (outward) form of religion and jurisprudential rules not as an ultimate goal, but rather as a transitional stage on [the path to the Truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haqiqa) — a stage that was no longer strictly binding upon them.[^cite_note-78] However, for the ruling religious-political elites, this situation served as a necessary rhetorical tool and an instrument of power for consolidating the masses ([the awâm](24710172.md)), maintaining social order, and ensuring the continuity of their rule. Preachers, meanwhile, recounted fabricated accounts of [Muhammad’s journey to Hell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra'_and_Mi'raj) and [hagiographic tales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography) attributed to the Companions and saints, all to emphasize the terrible fate awaiting those who abandoned rules and norms.[^cite_note-79]





Coins of the [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) (left),

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbumBatesFloor2012-80]

&#x20;which feature the addition of the brief Arabic phrase [Bism-Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basmala) alongside the imagery on [Sasanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian) coins, as well metal coins surviving from the Abbasids (right), reflect the history of the transition toward an approach in [Islamic thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_thought) that [strongly condemned foreign religious symbols and imagery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniconism).

While uprisings of varying scales throughout history have challenged the implementation of Sharia —or the manner of its application— at different levels of intensity, some rebellions were driven by the argument that Sharia was not being implemented sufficiently or correctly. Fueled by historical defeat against the West, domestic authoritarianism, and systemic injustice, contemporary [Islamist movements](15012.md) strive to impose an all-encompassing Sharia order modeled on a pristine 'Age of Bliss.' They operate along a spectrum that ranges from manufacturing consent through grass-roots social services to asserting control via political [violence and terrorism](566821.md). Sharia proponents are not obliged to speak the truth in every situation, as general [Islamic ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_ethics) would recommend; they have the right to conceal their identity and resort to [taqiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqiyya) in certain circumstances. Jan Michiel Otto summarizes the different meanings that can be attributed to this term in current literature and discourse.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto20089–10-81] Some researchers point to the profound changes Sharia has undergone from the time of Muhammad and the early caliphs —which themselves may have been shaped by retrospective attributions[^cite_note-82]— to the present day.

- *Divine, abstract sharia*: In this sense, Sharia is a rather abstract concept which leaves ample room for various interpretations.

- *Classical sharia*: The body of Islamic rules, principles and *[Ijtihād](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtih%C4%81d)* cases compiled by religious scholars during the first two centuries after Muhammad.

- *Historical sharia(s)*: Includes the entire body of all principles, rules, cases and interpretations developed and transmitted throughout a history of more than one thousand years across the entire Muslim world, since the closing of the gate of free interpretation up to the present.

- *Contemporary sharia(s)*: Contains the full spectrum of principles, rules, cases and interpretations developed and applied at present that migration, [modernisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_modernism), information and communication have decreased the dominance of the legal schools on understanding. (See:[Codification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codification_\(law\)))

## Establishment / Classical period

Sharia literature operates through a sequence of objectives, sources, and instruments that often have fluid, overlapping relationships with one another; an objective may be viewed as a source or an instrument, and an instrument as a source. Classical jurisprudence has been described as "one of the major intellectual achievements of Islam"[^cite_note-Lewis-ME-223-83] and its importance in Islam has been compared to that of [theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theology) in [Christianity](5211.md).[^cite_note-86] Nevertheless, confusion persists —even today —regarding fundamental issues that pave the way for state intervention in the daily lives of individuals, such as the definition or classification of beliefs and attitudes, as legal matters —encompassing muamelat (social and civil transactions) and ukubat (penal provisions)— that entail responsibility against laws; and attittudes regarding faith, worship, ethics, clothing etc. a matter of counsel, but cannot be a subject of law or punishment.[^cite_note-87]

### Epistemological framework (Methodology, *uṣūl al-fiqh*)

Main articles: [Principles of Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence), [Fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh), and [Madhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhab)

Further information: [Quranic hermeneutics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_hermeneutics) and [Tafsir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tafsir)

Classical Islamic jurisprudence refers how to elaborate and interpret religious sources that are considered reliable within the framework of "procedural principles" within its context such as [linguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic) and "[rhetorical tools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetoric)" to derive judgments for new situations by taking into account certain purposes and mesalih. Textual phrases usually dealt with under simple antithetical headings: general and particular, command and prohibition, obscure and clear, truth and metaphor. It also comprises methods for establishing authenticity of hadith and for determining when the legal force of a scriptural passage is [abrogated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naskh_\(tafsir\)) by a passage revealed at a later date.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7]



Imam Shafi'i tomb in [Cairo](6293.md), died 820 CE/204 AH, writer of [Al-Risala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Risala_\(book\)) on [principles of jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usul_al-fiqh)

The sources of judgment in classical fiqh are roughly divided into two: Manqūlāt (transmitted sources; Quran and hadith) and Aqliyyāt (rational sources; ijma, qiyas, ijtihad and others).[^cite_note-88] Some of them (Aqliyyāt) are considered to be the product of [scholastic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholastic_theology) and [Aristotelian logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_logic).[^cite_note-89] It was an important area of debate among traditional fiqh scholars how much space should be given to rational methods in creating provisions such as extracting provisions from religious texts, as well as expanding, restricting, abolishing or postponing these provisions according to new situations, considering the purpose and benefit, together with new sociologies, in the face of changing conditions.[^cite_note-90]

In this context, in the Classical period, the ulema were divided into groups (among other divisions such as political divisions) regarding the place of "['Aql](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/'Aql)" vis-à-vis naql:[^cite_note-91] those who rely on narration ([Atharists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atharism), [Ahl al-Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Hadith)), those who rely on reason ([Ahl al-Kalām](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam), [Mu'tazila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'tazila) and [Ahl al-Ra'y](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Ra'y)) and those who tried to find a middle way between the two attitudes such as [Abu al-Hasan al-Ash'ari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_al-Hasan_al-Ash'ari) in theology (syncretists).[^cite_note-auto-92] In the classical age of Islam, there were violent conflicts between rationalists (aqliyyun; [al-muʿtazila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'tazilism), [kalamiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam)) and traditionalist (naqliyyun, literalists, [Ahl al-Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Hadith)) groups and sects regarding the Quran and hadith or the place of reason in understanding the Quran and hadith,[^cite_note-Mirzaee,_A._2012_pp._7-26-93] as can be seen in the [Mihna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihna) example.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-Oubrou-95] Although the rationalists initially seemed to gain the upper hand in this conflict, with the rise of literalism, the Mutazila sank into history and literalism continued to live by gaining supporters.[^cite_note-Mirzaee,_A._2012_pp._7-26-93]

The formulation of the Sunni view on topic can be summarized as follows; Human reason is a gift from God which should be exercised to its fullest capacity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a15-96] However, use of reason alone is insufficient [to distinguish right from wrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enjoining_good_and_forbidding_wrong), and rational argumentation must draw its content from the body of transcendental knowledge revealed in the Quran and through the sunnah of Muhammad.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlén2003chpt._4c-11][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a15-96] In addition to the Quran and sunnah, the classical theory of Sunni fiqh recognizes two other sources of law: juristic consensus (*[ijmaʿ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)*) and analogical reasoning (*[qiyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas)*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72] It therefore studies the application and limits of analogy, as well as the value and limits of consensus, along with other methodological principles, some of which are accepted by only certain legal schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] This interpretive apparatus is brought together under the rubric of *[ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)*, which refers to a jurist's exertion in an attempt to arrive at a ruling on a particular question.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7]

The theory of [Twelver Shia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shi'ism) jurisprudence parallels that of Sunni schools with some differences, such as recognition of reason (*[ʿaql](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/'Aql)*) as a source of law in place of *qiyas* and extension of the notion of sunnah to include traditions of the [imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Imams).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlén2003chpt._4c-11][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamali1999121–22-97]

#### Aims of Sharia and public interest

Main articles: [Maqasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqasid) and [Maslaha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslaha)



Mausoleum of [Al-Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghazali) in [Tus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tus,_Iran); Many see Al-Ghazali as the last [mujtahid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujtahid), and his period as the period when the door of ijtihad closed and, in a sense, the Islamic Middle Ages began, and they think that Ghazali is responsible for this.

[^cite_note-98]

*Maqāṣid* (aims or purposes) of Sharia and *maṣlaḥa* (welfare or public interest) are two related classical doctrines which have come to play an increasingly prominent role in modern times.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuderija20142–6-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2009-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGleave2012-101] [Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghaz%C4%81l%C4%AB), [Izz al-Din ibn 'Abd al-Salam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izz_al-Din_ibn_'Abd_al-Salam) and [Abu Ishaq al-Shatibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ishaq_al-Shatibi) used maslaha and madasıd as equivalent terms.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] Synonyms for the term maqāṣid aš-šarīʿa are the expressions maqāṣid aš-šāriʿ ("intentions of the legislature"), maqāṣid at-tašrīʿ ("intentions of the legislature "), ruḥ aš -šarīʿa ("Spirit of Sharia"), ḥikmat at-tašrīʿ ("Wisdom of Legislation") and falsafat at-tašrīʿ ("Philosophy of Legislation").[^cite_note-104]

They were first clearly articulated by [al-Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghazali) (d. 1111), who argued that *Maqāṣid* and *maslaha* was God's general purpose in revealing the divine law, and that its specific aim was preservation of five essentials of human well-being: religion, life, intellect, offspring, and property.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpwis200765-105]

Although most classical-era jurists recognized *maslaha* and *maqasid* as important legal principles, they held different views regarding the role they should play in Islamic law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuderija20142–6-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGleave2012-101] Some jurists viewed them as auxiliary rationales constrained by scriptural sources and analogical reasoning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuderija20142–6-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpwis200766–68-106] Others regarded them as an "independent" source of law, whose general principles could override specific [inferences based on the letter of scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zahiri_school).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuderija20142–6-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpwis200768–69-107] Taking maqasid and maslaha as an "independent" [source of sharia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sources_of_sharia) – rather than an auxiliary one – will pave the way for the re-critique and reorganization of [ahkam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam) in the context of maqasid and maslaha,[^cite_note-files.eric.ed.gov-108] thus (including [hudud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud)), which is often criticized in terms of today's values and seen as problematic,[^cite_note-Gontowska,_Luiza_Maria_2005-109] in terms of the purposes of Sharia and social benefits will be replaced by new ones. [Abdallah bin Bayyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdallah_bin_Bayyah) goes further with an approach that prioritizes purpose and benefit among the sources of Sharia and declares it to be the heart of "usul-al fiqh".[^cite_note-files.eric.ed.gov-108]

While the latter view was held by a minority of classical jurists, in modern times it came to be championed in different forms by prominent scholars who sought to adapt Islamic law to changing social conditions by drawing on the intellectual heritage of traditional jurisprudence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuderija20142–6-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2009-100] These scholars expanded the inventory of *maqasid* to include such aims of Sharia as reform and [women's rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights) ([Rashid Rida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Rida)); justice and freedom ([Mohammed al-Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_al-Ghazali)); and [human rights](13831.md) and [dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dignity) ([Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuderija20142–6-99]

#### Sources

Main article: [Sources of Sharia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sources_of_Sharia)

Various sources of Islamic Laws are used by [Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh) to elaborate the body of [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic) law, which are called **Masdar** (مصادر) or **Dalil** (دليل).[^cite_note-Jurisprudence-110] Islamic scholar [Rashid Rida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Rida) (1865–1935 CE) lists the four basic sources of Islamic law, agreed upon by all [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam): "the \[well-known] sources of legislation in Islam are four: the *[Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran)*, the *[Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah)*, the [consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma) of the *[ummah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah)* and *[ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)* undertaken by competent jurists".[^cite_note-111] While traditional understanding strongly denies that Quran may have changed,([Al Hejr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Hejr)\:9)[^cite_note-112] the authenticity of hadiths could only be questioned through the chain of narration, though some western researchers suggests that primary sources may have also been evolved.

- *[Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran)*: in Islam, the Quran is considered to be the most sacred source of law.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a16–18-113]

  &#x20;According to classical mainstream jurists, the verses of the Quran that were "revealed later" in Islamic language may have [restricted or abolished the earlier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naskh_\(tafsir\)) verses.

  [^cite_note-JBSILITA1990\:43-44,56-59,122-124-114]

  &#x20;Therefore, deciding which verses of the Quran will be used, in addition to other knowledge and skills, may be the job of [lawyers who know these issues in detail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama). Whether the Sunnah could limit the Quran remained a matter of debate. [Fiqh sects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhab) are schools of understanding that try to determine the actions that people should do or avoid based on the Quran and hadiths. [Hanafi sect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi_school) requires that in order for something to be considered [fard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard), it must be clearly commanded in the Qur'an "according to logical and grammatical requirements such as addressee, order and scope, etc.", expressions that do not meet this condition are placed in the "wujub" class, which expresses a lower level of necessity.

  [^cite_note-115]

  &#x20;Some of these fiqh results (ahkam) may also indicate exaggeration of statements, generalizations taken out of context, and imperative broadening of scope.



A page from the [Sanaa manuscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanaa_manuscript). According to the [Quranists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranists), it was the only reliable source of religion, preserved from corruption. However, certain [Quranic studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_studies) have led to the proposal of theses that radically alter the traditional narrative regarding its [origins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahruf), development, and [canonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization_of_Islamic_scripture).

[\[note 8\]](#cite_note-118)

A small number of verses in the Quran are about general rules of governance, inheritance, [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_Islam), [crime and punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud). There are notable exceptions to some of these rules, as in [Al-Ahzab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ahzab) 49; jurists state that they are specific to Muhammad alone. Although the Quran does not impose [a specific legal-management system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_aspects_of_Islam), it emphasizes [custom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ruf) in nearly 40 verses and commands [justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adl). ([An-Nahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An-Nahl); 90) The practices prescribed in the Quran are considered as reflections of [contextual legal understandings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_hermeneutics), as can be clearly seen in some examples such as [Qisas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas) and [diya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\)).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] The following statement in the Quran is thought to be the general rule of testimony in [Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_jurisprudence), except for crime and punishment – for example, debt, shopping, etc.; O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing....with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her....[^cite_note-121] Rules of inheritance was also mentioned in the Quran, in regards to certain family members having their share..<sup>\[Quran [4\:11](https://quran.com/4?startingVerse=11)]</sup>[^cite_note-davidpowers-122]

In a different example, in [the necklace story of Aisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Necklace_Incident), called [Asbab al-Nuzul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asbab_al-Nuzul) for surah [An-Nur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An-Nur) \:11-20 four witnesses were required for the accusation of adultery. In addition, those who made accusations that did not meet the specified conditions would be punished with 80 lashes. The jurisprudence of later periods stipulates that witnesses must be men, covering all [hadd crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud) and people who did not have credibility and honesty in society ([slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery), non-adl; [sinners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasiq), infidels) could not testify against believers.[^cite_note-123] In addition, the Islamic judiciary did not require proof of the issues defined as [tazir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazir).[^cite_note-124] The statement in the Qur'an that determines the status of slaves in community is; *ma malakat aymanuhum*[^cite_note-auto1-125] meaning "[those whom your right hands possess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery)".

Of the few criminal cases listed as crimes in the Quran, only a few of them are punished by the classical books of Sharia as determined by the verses of the Quran and are called [hudud laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud_laws). How the verse [Al-Ma'idah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ma'idah) 33, which describes the crime of [hirabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirabah), should be understood is a matter of debate even today.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-AutoN0-18-127] The verse talks about the punishment of criminals by killing, hanging, having their [hands and feet cut off on opposite sides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-amputation), and being exiled from the earth, in response to an -abstract- crime such as "[fighting against Allah and His Messenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasad)". Today, commentators – in the face of the development of the understanding of law and the increasing reactions to corporal punishment[^cite_note-128] – claim that the verse determines the punishment of "concrete sequential criminal acts" – such as massacre, robbery and rape – in addition to rebellion against the legitimate government, and that the punishment to be given depends on the existence of these preconditions.

- *[Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah)* / *[Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith)*: Although hadiths have largely replaced the sunnah in [orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) legislation today, according to some research, the opposite was true in the early Islamic society. Sunnah originally meant a tradition that did not contain the definition of good and bad.

  [^cite_note-Juynboll-129]

  [^cite_note-130]

  [^cite_note-131]

  [^cite_note-OISO-132]

  [^cite_note-Oxford_University_Press-133]

  &#x20;Later, "good traditions" ([Ma'ruf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ruf)) began to be referred to as sunnah and the concept of "Muhammad's sunnah" was established.

  [^cite_note-Juynboll-129]

  &#x20;Muhammad's sunnah gave way to the "hadiths of Muhammad" which were [transmitted orally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition),

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown20093-134]

  &#x20;then recorded in corpuses and [systematized and purified within following centuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_studies). According to Harald Motzki and Daniel W. Brown the earliest Islamic legal reasonings that have come down to us were "virtually hadith-free", but gradually, over the course of second century [A.H](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijri_year). "the infiltration and incorporation of Prophetic hadiths into Islamic jurisprudence" took place.

  [^cite_note-135]

  [^cite_note-DWBRTMIT1996\:12-136]

  &#x20;The value of customs (see also: [urf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urf), [ma'ruf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ruf)) manifests itself in the classification of food and drinks as [halal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halal) and [haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram); Some jurists such as Al- Shafi'i and [Ibn Qudamah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Qudamah) have determined the haram/halal criterion as "compatibility or contradiction with the Arab's customary habits and nature".

  [^cite_note-137]

Although for many [Muslim sects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhab), hadith was second to the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) in authority,[^cite_note-EB-138] the majority of [Sharia rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam) were derived from hadith rather than the Quran.[^cite_note-Forte-1978-2-85][^cite_note-140] The body of hadith provides more detailed and practical legal guidance, but it was recognized early on that not all of them were authentic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a16–18-113] Hadith compilations were developed along divergent lines influenced by distinct cultural spheres where each sect established over time its own accredited corpus, and shaped by the presuppositions of religio-political divisions such as Sunni, Shia, and [Kharijite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijite); this situation, which compromised reliability, subsequently paved the way for scholars of hadith to establish specific criteria for [verifying the authenticity of these narrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_terminology). Later Islamic scholars developed various personal criteria for evaluating their authenticity by assessing the trustworthiness of the individuals listed in their transmission chains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a16–18-113] These studies narrowed down the vast corpus of prophetic traditions to several thousand "sound (seeming to collectors)" hadiths, which were collected in several sect-specific canonical compilations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a16–18-113] The hadiths which enjoyed concurrent transmission were deemed [mutawatir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutawatir); however, the vast majority of hadiths were handed down by only one or a few transmitters and were therefore seen to yield only probable knowledge.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a16–18-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72] The uncertainty was further compounded by the ambiguity of the language contained in some hadiths and Quranic passages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a16–18-113]

In [Imam Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam_Malik)'s usage, hadith did not consist only of the words claimed to belong to Muhammad as is the case with [Shiite Muslims](26961.md). While hadith does not appear to be an important source of decision for early fiqh scholars such as [Abu Hanifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hanifa), for later scholars, hadith is perceived as the words of Muhammad merely and is considered as a strong and separate source of decision alongside the Quran. Today, [Quranists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranism) do not consider hadiths as a valid source of religious rulings.[^cite_note-\:3-141][^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

- *[Ijma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)*: it is the consensus that could in principle elevate a ruling based on probable evidence to absolute certainty.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a21–22-144]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

  &#x20;This classical doctrine drew its authority from a series of hadiths stating that the Islamic community could never agree on an error.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a21–22-144]

  &#x20;This form of consensus was technically defined as agreement of all competent jurists in any particular generation, acting as representatives of the community.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a21–22-144]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamali1999146-145]

  &#x20;However, the practical difficulty of obtaining and ascertaining such an agreement meant that it had little impact on legal development.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a21–22-144]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

  &#x20;A more pragmatic form of consensus, which could be determined by consulting works of prominent jurists, was used to confirm a ruling so that it could not be reopened for further discussion.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

  &#x20;The cases for which there was a consensus account form less than 1 percent of the body of classical jurisprudence.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a21–22-144]

- *[Qiyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas)*: it is the Analogical reasoning that is used to derive a ruling for a situation not addressed in the scripture by analogy with a scripturally based rule.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72]

  &#x20;In a classic example, the Quranic prohibition of drinking wine is extended to all intoxicating substances, on the basis of the "cause" (*ʿilla*) shared by these situations, which in this case is identified to be intoxication.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72]

  &#x20;Since the cause of a rule may not be apparent, its selection commonly occasioned controversy and extensive debate.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a23–24-146]

  &#x20;Majority of [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslims) view *[Qiyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas)* as a central Pillar of *[Ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)*.

  [^cite_note-overview-147]

  &#x20;On the other hand; [Zahirites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zahiri), [Ahmad ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Hanbal), [Al-Bukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Bukhari), early [Hanbalites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbalites), etc rejected *Qiyas* amongst the Sunnis.

  [^cite_note-148]

  [^cite_note-149]

  [^cite_note-150]

  &#x20;Twelver Shia jurisprudence also does not recognize the use of *qiyas*, but relies on reason (*ʿ['aql](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/'aql)*) in its place.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlén2003chpt._4c-11]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneider2014-12]

#### Intellectual exertion

Main article: [Ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)

***Ijtihad*** lit. 'physical'  or 'mental effort'[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151] refers to independent reasoning by an expert in [Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_law),[^cite_note-ODI-Ijtihad-152] or exertion of a jurist's mentality in finding a solution to a legal question[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151] in contrast with *[taqlid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqlid)* ([conformity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conformity) to precedent ijtihad).[^cite_note-ODI-Ijtihad-152][^cite_note-153] According to theory, *ijtihad* requires expertise in the Arabic language, theology, religious texts, and principles of jurisprudence (*[usul al-fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence)*),[^cite_note-ODI-Ijtihad-152] and is not employed where authentic and trusted texts ([Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an) and [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith)) are considered unambiguous with regard to the question, or where there is an existing scholarly consensus (*[ijma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151] An [Islamic scholar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_scholar) who perform *ijtihad* is called "*[mujtahid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad#Qualifications_of_a_mujtahid)*".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151][^cite_note-154] In the general understanding, beyond the limitation of ijtihad to those situations that do not have a clear ruling in the Quran and hadiths, scholars who have the ability to give general judgments are also ranked with definitions such as "mujtahid mutlaq", "mujtahid in sect", "mujtahid in issue".[^cite_note-155] Rulings based on ijtihad are not decisions that require obligatory implementation for other Muslims.

Throughout the first five [Islamic centuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calendar), *ijtihad* continued to practise amongst Sunni Muslims. The controversy surrounding *ijtihad* started with the beginning of the twelfth century.[^cite_note-B._Hallaq_20,_33-156] By the 14th century, [Islamic Fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh) prompted leading Sunni jurists to state that the main legal questions had been addressed and then *ijtihad* was gradually restricted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151] In the modern era, this gave rise to a perception amongst [Orientalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orientalism) scholars and sections of the Muslim public that the so-called "gate of *ijtihad*" was closed at the start of the classical era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151][^cite_note-157]



[Mustafa Öztürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_%C3%96zt%C3%BCrk) follows [Fazlur Rahman Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlur_Rahman_Malik)'s footsteps and says that the verses are revealed on [the historical context](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_hermeneutics), the [Ahkam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam) are not among the essence and [purposes of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqasid), with an example: [Slaves were considered property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_concubinage); could be bought, sold, rented and shared.

[^cite_note-jebro-158]

[^cite_note-159]

&#x20;[Al-Sarakhsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Sarakhsi) decided that the paternity determination of the child to be born could be made by [draw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lottery), and asks how many of you can accept this understanding today?

[^cite_note-160]

Starting from the 18th century, [Islamic reformers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival) began calling for abandonment of *taqlid* and emphasis on *ijtihad*, which they saw as a return to Islamic origins.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151] The advocacy of *ijtihad* has been particularly associated with [Islamic Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Modernism) and [Salafiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi) movements. Among contemporary Muslims in the West there have emerged new visions of *ijtihad* which emphasize substantive moral values over traditional juridical understandings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151]

[Shia](26961.md) jurists did not use the term *ijtihad* until the 12th century. With the exception of [Zaydis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaydi), the early [Imami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_in_Shia_doctrine) [Shia](26961.md) were unanimous in censuring *Ijtihad* in the field of law (*[Ahkam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam)*) until the Shiite embrace of various doctrines of [Mu'tazila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu%CA%BFtazila) and classical Sunnite *[Fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009c-151][^cite_note-161] After the victory of the *[Usulis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usuli)* who based law on principles ([usul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usul_Fiqh_in_Ja'fari_school)) over the *[Akhbaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhbari)* ("traditionalists") who emphasized reports or traditions (*khabar*) by the 19th century, *Ijtihad* would become a mainstream Shia practice.[^cite_note-Mohammad_Farzaneh_2015_6-17]

The classical process of ijtihad combined these generally recognized principles with other methods, which were not adopted by all legal schools, such as *[istihsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istihsan)* (juristic preference), *[istislah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istislah)* (consideration of public interest) and *[istishab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istishab)* (presumption of continuity).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009-72]

Considering that, [as a rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_Islamic_jurisprudence), there was a hierarchy and power ranking among the sources of Sharia;[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163] for example, a subcategory or an auxiliary source will not be able to eliminate a provision clearly stated in the main source or prohibit a practice that was not prohibited though it was known and practiced during the prophetic period. If we look at an example such as the abolition of the validity of [Mut'a marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mut'a_marriage), is touched upon in the [Quran 4\:24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran_4\:24),[^cite_note-164] and not prohibited (Sunnis translate the words used in the relevant verse with terms used to describe the ordinary marriage event) according to Sunnis is banned by [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) towards the end of his lifetime,[^cite_note-165] and according to [Shiites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiites), by [Omar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar), "according to his own opinion" and relying on power. The Shiite sect did not accept the jurisprudence of Omar, whose political and religious authority they rejected from the beginning.
