| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Fiqh)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Fiqh)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Fiqh) |

Main articles: [Ahkam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam), [Hadd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadd), and [Tazir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazir)

Further information: [Status of women's testimony in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_of_women's_testimony_in_Islam) and [Women in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam)

Fiqh is concerned with ethical standards as much as with legal norms, seeking to establish not only what is and is not legal, but also what is morally right and wrong.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoulsonEl_Shamsy2019-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010145-16] Sharia rulings fall into one of five categories known as "the five decisions" (*al-aḥkām al-khamsa*): [mandatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard) (*farḍ* or *wājib*), [recommended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustahabb) (*mandūb* or *mustaḥabb*), [neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubah) (*mubāḥ*), [reprehensible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makruh) (*makrūh*), and [forbidden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram) (*ḥarām*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneider2014-12]

It is a sin or a crime to perform a forbidden action or not to perform a mandatory action.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Reprehensible acts should be avoided, but they are not considered to be sinful or punishable in court.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a20-166] Avoiding reprehensible acts and performing recommended acts is held to be subject of reward in the afterlife, while neutral actions entail no judgment from God.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a20-166] Jurists disagree on whether the term *[ḥalāl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halal)* covers the first three or the first four categories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] The legal and moral verdict depends on whether the action is committed out of necessity (*ḍarūra*) and on the underlying intention (*[niyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niyya)*), as expressed in the legal maxim "acts are \[evaluated according] to intention."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

[Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) fiqh does not consider both terms as synonymous and makes a distinction between "[fard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard)" and "[wajib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wajib)"; In Hanafi fiqh, two conditions are required to impose the fard rule. 1. [Nass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nass_\(Islam\)), (only verses of the Qur'an can be accepted as evidence here, not [hadiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith)) 2.The expression of the text referring to the subject must be clear and precise enough not to allow other interpretations. The term wajib is used for situations that do not meet the second of these conditions.[^cite_note-167] However, this understanding may not be sufficient to explain every situation. For example, Hanafis accept [5 daily prayers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah) as fard. However, some religious groups such as [Quranists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranists) and [Shiites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiites), who do not doubt that the Quran existing today is a religious source, infer from the same verses that it is clearly ordered to pray 2 or 3 times,[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] not 5 times. In addition, in religious literature, wajib is widely used for all kinds of religious requirements, without expressing any fiqh definition.

As seen above and in many other examples, classifications and labels have a relative character shaped by the understanding of the people and groups who make them. For example, believing in the existence and miracles of [Awliya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali) is presented as a "condition" for orthodox Islam by many prominent Sunni creed writers such as [Al-Tahawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Tahawi) and [Nasafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hafs_Umar_al-Nasafi)[^cite_note-Jonathan_A.C._Brown_2012_p._123-172][^cite_note-Christopher_Taylor_1999_pp._5-6-173] and is accepted in traditional Sunnis and Shi'ism. However, this understanding, along with expressions of respect and visits to the graves of saints, are seen as unacceptable [heresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy) by puritanical and revivalist Islamic movements such as [Salafism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafism), [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism) and [Islamic Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Modernism).[^cite_note-ReferenceA-174]



13th century slave market, during the era of [slavery in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Yemen). see also: [Islamic views on concubinage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_concubinage).

About six verses address [the way a woman should dress](68301.md) when in public;[^cite_note-bucar-175] Muslim scholars have differed as how to understand these verses, with some stating that a Hijab is a command (fard) to be fulfilled[^cite_note-Hameed-176][^cite_note-181] and others say simply not.[^cite_note-Asra-2015-182][^cite_note-184]

Islamic primary law sources, like the legal systems of classical antiquity and the ancient Near East, does not contain a true equivalent of the modern concept of rape, which is in turn based on the modern notions of individual autonomy and inviolability of the body, particularly the female body. [Classical jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh), attempted to fill this gap by likening rape to defined crimes such as [adultery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zina) and [hirabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirabah). In some cases, this approach has had detrimental consequences for the victims. In Afghanistan and Dubai, some women who made accusations of rape have been charged with fornication or adultery.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] The statement in the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an) that determines the status of slaves and concubines in the understanding of Sharia is as follows; *ma malakat aymanuhum* or *milk al-yamin*[^cite_note-auto1-125] meaning "[those whom your right hands possess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery)". Hence, sexual use of female slaves was considered within of their definition that "they are the well-earned rewards or properties of their masters", and with a few exceptions,[^cite_note-191]

A special religious decision, which is "specific to" a person, group, institution, event, situation, belief and practice in different areas of life, and usually includes the approval/disapproval of a judgment, is called [fatwa](11259.md). [Tazir penalties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazir), which are outside the [Qisas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas) and [Hudud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud) laws, have not been codified, and their discretion and implementation are under the initiative and authority of the judge or political authority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009c-192] [Mustafa Öztürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_%C3%96zt%C3%BCrk) points out some another developments in the [Islamic creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqidah), leading changes in ahkam such as determining the conditions of [takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir) according to [theologians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam); First Muslims believed that God [lived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-%E1%B8%A4ayy) in [the sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Heavens) as [Ahmad Ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Ibn_Hanbal) says: "Whoever says that Allah is everywhere is a [heretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heretic), an infidel, should be invited to repent, but if he does not, be killed." This understanding changes later and gives way to the understanding that "God cannot be assigned a place and He is everywhere."[^cite_note-193]

Judgment that concerns individuals is personal and, for example, in an Islamic Qisas or [compensation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\)) decisions, jurist must take into account "personal labels" such as the [gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_islam), [freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery), religious and [social status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_status) such as [mu'min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'min), [kafir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafir), [musta'min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musta'min), [dhimmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi), [apostate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostate), etc. Similar distinctions also apply to witnessing practices, which have a fundamental value in the establishment of judicial provisions, such as the identification of the criminals. Islamic preachers constantly emphasize the importance of [adalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adl), and in trials, the judge is not expected to observe equality among those on trial, but is expected to act fairly or balanced. Traditional fiqh states that legal and religious responsibility begins with [rushd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baligh).[^cite_note-194]

### Topics and details (*furūʿ al-fiqh*)

Main articles: [Topics in Sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topics_in_Sharia_law) and [Fatwa](11259.md)

Further information: [Islamic criminal jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_jurisprudence)

Further information: [Capital punishment in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Islam) and [Rape in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_in_Islam)

The domain of *furūʿ al-fiqh* (lit. branches of fiqh) is traditionally divided into *ʿibādāt* (rituals or acts of worship) and *muʿāmalāt* (social relations).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] Many jurists further divided the body of substantive jurisprudence into "the four quarters", called rituals, sales, marriage and injuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a28–30-195] Each of these terms figuratively stood for a variety of subjects.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a28–30-195] For example, the quarter of sales would encompass partnerships, guaranty, gifts, and bequests, among other topics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a28–30-195] Juristic works were arranged as a sequence of such smaller topics, each called a "book" (*kitab*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a28–30-195] The special significance of ritual was marked by always placing its discussion at the start of the work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a28–30-195]

Some historians distinguish a field of [Islamic criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_law), which combines several traditional categories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009c-192][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneider2014-12] Several crimes with scripturally prescribed punishments are known as *[hudud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Jurists developed various restrictions which in many cases made them virtually impossible to apply.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Other crimes involving intentional bodily harm are judged according to a version of *[lex talionis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lex_talionis)* that prescribes a punishment analogous to the crime (*[qisas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas)*), but the victims or their heirs may accept a monetary compensation (*[diya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\))*) or pardon the perpetrator instead; only *diya* is imposed for non-intentional harm.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009c-192] Other criminal cases belong to the category of *[taʿzīr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazir)*, where the goal of punishment is correction or rehabilitation of the culprit and its form is largely left to [the judge's discretion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_discretion).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009c-192]

Which crime falls into which category may vary depending on understanding. For example, according to some verses and hadiths, those who avoided [alms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat) and [prayers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah) were the ones to be fought against,[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-zakat-197]) and according to some understandings, they were [people who had abandoned religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam) – theologians debated whether worship was a part of faith – and according to classical [fiqh sects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_schools_and_branches), they were people who should be killed.[^cite_note-198] However, even if today's dominant understanding defines the abandonment of worship as [sinfulness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasiq), does not approve of giving worldly punishment for them. However, in Sharia governments, their testimony against a devout Muslim may not be accepted, they may be humiliated and barred from certain positions because of this tag. In practice, since early on in Islamic history, criminal cases were usually handled by ruler-administered courts or local police using procedures which were only loosely related to Sharia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiadeh2009c-192]

The two major genres of *furūʿ* literature are the *[mukhtasar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhtasar)* (concise summary of law) and the *mabsut* (extensive commentary).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] *Mukhtasars* were short specialized treatises or general overviews that could be used in a classroom or consulted by judges.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a10–11-199] A *mabsut*, which usually provided a commentary on a *mukhtasar* and could stretch to dozens of large volumes, recorded alternative rulings with their justifications, often accompanied by a proliferation of cases and conceptual distinctions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a10–11-199] The terminology of juristic literature was conservative and tended to preserve notions which had lost their practical relevance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] At the same time, the cycle of abridgement and commentary allowed jurists of each generation to articulate a modified body of law to meet changing social conditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a10–11-199] Other juristic genres include the *qawāʿid* (succinct formulas meant to aid the student remember general principles) and collections of fatwas by a particular scholar.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

### Schools of law and characteristics

Main articles: [Madhhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhhab) and [Politics and Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_and_Islam)



Juristic exchange between [Abu Dawood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dawud_al-Sijistani) and [Ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Hanbal). One of the oldest literary manuscripts of the Islamic world, dated October 879 AD.

Schools of Islamic law (madhhabs) are not static, rigid texts resulting from pre-conceived theories. Juristic thought gradually developed in study circles, -later referred to as schools of law- where independent scholars met to learn from a local master and discuss religious topics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014125-74][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a31–35-76] At first, these circles were fluid in their membership, but with time distinct regional legal schools crystallized around shared sets of methodological principles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a31–35-76] The distinctive character of each school was shaped by the geography and socio-cultural environment in which it emerged, as well as by the crises of the era and interactions with political authorities. These structures are distinguished from one another by their unique epistemological stances, methodological choices, and interpretive frameworks. Ultimately, traditionalist, rationalist, and synthesizing approaches—along with text-oriented/reactionary or political leanings—constitute the primary elements that define the fundamental identity and fabric of these schools. As the boundaries of the schools became clearly delineated, the authority of their doctrinal tenets came to be vested in a master jurist from earlier times, who was henceforth identified as the school's founder.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a31–35-76] In the course of the first three centuries of Islam, all legal schools came to accept the broad outlines of classical legal theory, according to which Islamic law had to be firmly rooted in the Quran and hadith.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014130-200]

The main Sunni schools of law are the [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi), [Maliki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maliki), [Shafi'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi'i) and [Hanbali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali) madhhabs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] They emerged in the ninth and tenth centuries and by the twelfth century almost all jurists aligned themselves with a particular madhhab.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] These four schools recognize each other's validity and they have interacted in legal debate over the centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] Rulings of these schools are followed across the Muslim world without exclusive regional restrictions, but they each came to dominate in different parts of the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] For example, the Maliki school is predominant in North and West Africa; the Hanafi school in South and Central Asia; the Shafi'i school in Lower Egypt, East Africa, and Southeast Asia; and the Hanbali school in North and Central Arabia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] The first centuries of Islam also witnessed a number of short-lived Sunni madhhabs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] The [Zahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zahiri) school, which is commonly identified as extinct, continues to exert influence over legal thought.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] The development of Shia legal schools occurred along the lines of theological differences of [Imamate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_in_Shia_doctrine) and resulted in formation of the [Twelver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver), [Zaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaidiyyah) and [Ismaili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismaili) madhhabs, whose differences from Sunni legal schools are roughly of the same order as the differences among Sunni schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] The Ibadi school —a distinct school of jurisprudence that emerged from the [Kharijite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijite) movement, which began as a radical rebellion against the elitism and rule of lineage (whether the Sunni tradition developing under the Umayyad and Abbasid dynasties rooted in the [Quraysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quraysh), or the Shi'a tradition centered on the [Ahl al-Bayt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Bayt))[^cite_note-204]— is predominant in Oman[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] and distinguishes itself from other schools by advocating for an elective and accountable imamate.

## Pre-modern Islamic legal system

### Jurists

Main articles: [Mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti) and [Madrasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa)

Sharia was traditionally interpreted by [muftis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti). During the first few centuries of Islam, muftis were private legal specialists who normally also held other jobs. They issued [fatwas](11259.md) (legal opinions), generally free of charge, in response to questions from laypersons or requests for consultation coming from judges, which would be stated in general terms. Fatwas were regularly upheld in courts, and when they were not, it was usually because the fatwa was contradicted by a more authoritative legal opinion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a9–10,_13-205] The stature of jurists was determined by their scholarly reputation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a9–10-206][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013499-207] The majority of classical legal works, written by author-jurists, were based in large part on fatwas of distinguished muftis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a9–10-206] These fatwas functioned as a form of legal [precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedent), unlike court verdicts, which were valid only for the given case.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010180-208] Although independent muftis never disappeared, from the 12th century onward Muslim rulers began to appoint salaried muftis to answer questions from the public.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013501-209] Over the centuries, [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) muftis were gradually incorporated into state bureaucracies, while [Shia](26961.md) jurists in Iran progressively asserted an autonomous authority starting from the early modern era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210]



[Ulugh Beg Madrasa, Samarkand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulugh_Beg_Madrasa,_Samarkand) (est. 1422)

Islamic law was initially taught in study circles that gathered in mosques and private homes. The teacher, assisted by advanced students, provided commentary on concise treatises of law and examined the students' understanding of the text. This tradition continued to be practiced in *[madrasas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa)*, which spread during the 10th and 11th centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a45–47-211][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014217-212] Madrasas were institutions of higher learning devoted principally to study of law, but also offering other subjects such as theology, medicine, and mathematics. The madrasa complex usually consisted of a mosque, boarding house, and a library. It was maintained by a *[waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)* (charitable endowment), which paid salaries of professors, stipends of students, and defrayed the costs of construction and maintenance. At the end of a course, the professor granted a license (*[ijaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijaza)*) certifying a student's competence in its subject matter.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014217-212] Students specializing in law would complete a curriculum consisting of preparatory studies, the doctrines of a particular madhhab, and training in legal disputation, and finally write a dissertation, which earned them a license to teach and issue fatwas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013501-209][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a45–47-211]

### Courts

Main articles: [Judge (Islamic law)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judge_\(Islamic_law\)), [Mazalim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazalim), [Shurta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shurta), and [Muhtasib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhtasib)



The poet [Saadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadi_Shirazi) and a [dervish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dervish) go to settle their quarrel before a judge (16th century Persian miniature).

A [judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judge_\(Islamic_law\)) (qadi) was in charge of the qadi's court (*mahkama*), also called the Sharia court. Qadis were trained in Islamic law, though not necessarily to a level required to issue fatwas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a11–12-213] Court personnel also included a number of assistants performing various roles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010158-214] Judges were theoretically independent in their decisions, though they were appointed by the ruler and often experienced pressure from members of the ruling elite where their interests were at play.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013501-209] The role of qadis was to evaluate the evidence, establish the facts of the case, and issue a verdict based on the applicable rulings of Islamic jurisprudence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] The qadi was supposed to solicit a fatwa from a mufti if it was unclear how the law should be applied to the case.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009-215] Since Islamic legal theory does not recognize the distinction between private and public law, court procedures were identical for civil and criminal cases, and required a private plaintiff to produce evidence against the defendant. The main type of evidence was oral witness testimony. The standards of evidence for criminal cases were so strict that a conviction was often difficult to obtain even for apparently clear-cut cases.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Most historians believe that because of these stringent procedural norms, qadi's courts at an early date lost their jurisdiction over criminal cases, which were instead handled in other types of courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETillier2014-216]

If an accusation did not result in a verdict in a qadi's court, the plaintiff could often pursue it in another type of court called the *[mazalim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazalim)* court, administered by the ruler's council.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] The rationale for *mazalim* (lit. wrongs, grievances) courts was to address the wrongs that Sharia courts were unable to address, including complaints against government officials. Islamic jurists were commonly in attendance and a judge often presided over the court as a deputy of the ruler.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013501-209] *Mazalim* verdicts were supposed to conform to the spirit of Sharia, but they were not bound by the letter of the law or the procedural restrictions of qadi's courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009-215]

The police (*[shurta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shurta)*), which took initiative in preventing and investigating crime, operated its own courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013501-209] Like the mazalim courts, police courts were not bound by the rules of Sharia and had the powers to inflict discretionary punishments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETillier2014-216] Another office for maintaining public order was the *[muhtasib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhtasib)* (market inspector), who was charged with preventing fraud in economic transactions and infractions against public morality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013501-209] The *muhtasib* took an active role in pursuing these types of offenses and meted out punishments based on [local custom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urf).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETillier2014-216]

### Punishments

Further information: [Islamic criminal jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_jurisprudence)

Further information: [Cross-amputation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-amputation)

The classical Islamic legal tradition did not have a separate category for criminal law as does modern law.[^cite_note-whsha-217] The classical Islamic jurisprudence typically divided the subject matter of law into four "quarters", that is rituals, sales, marriage, and injuries.[^cite_note-mc13-218] In modern usage, Islamic criminal law has been extracted and collated from that classical Islamic jurisprudence literature into three categories of rules:[^cite_note-mc13-218] As crimes against God they cannot be forgiven but the punishments are seldom enforced as they can be averted by the slightest doubts or ambiguities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009b311-219]

- *Hadd* (literally "borders, boundaries, limits")

  [^cite_note-WATR-220]

  [^cite_note-221]

  &#x20;is applied to punishments (ranging from public lashing, [public stoning to death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning_in_Islam), [amputation of hands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-amputation), [crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion#In_Islam), depending on the crime),

  [^cite_note-OIS-Hadd-2012-222]

  &#x20;for a limited number of crimes ([murder](20171.md), [adultery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adultery), [slander](28661.md), [theft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theft), etc.),

  [^cite_note-zmh11-223]

  [^cite_note-224]

  &#x20;for which punishments have been determined (or traditionally thought to have been determined) in the verses of [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran).

- *Qisas*, (literally "retaliation in kind"

  [^cite_note-mohelawa-225]

  ) and *[diya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\))*, (دية) ("blood money"), in Islamic jurisprudence, are the second category of crimes, where Sharia specifies equal retaliation (*qisas*) or monetary compensation (*diya*),

  [^cite_note-226]

  &#x20;as a possible punishment. Included in this category is homicide, for example, which Islamic law treats as a civil dispute between believers.

  [^cite_note-227]

  &#x20;Qisas principle is available against the accused, to the victim or victim's heirs, when a Muslim is murdered, suffers bodily injury or suffers property damage.

  [^cite_note-228]

  &#x20;In the case of murder, qisas means the right of a murder victim's nearest relative or [wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali) (ولي) (legal guardian) to, if the court approves, take the life of the killer or do it in his behalf.

  [^cite_note-229]

- *Tazir* (literally "to punish",

  [^cite_note-WATR-220]

  &#x20;sometimes spelled as taazir, ti'zar, tazar, ta'azar) is the third category, and refers to offense mentioned in the Quran or the Hadiths, but where neither the Quran nor the Hadiths specify a punishment.

  [^cite_note-taziroup-230]

  [^cite_note-231]

  &#x20;In Tazir cases, the punishment is at the discretion of the state, the ruler, or a *[qadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadi)* (*kadi*),

  [^cite_note-twtazir1-232]

  [^cite_note-233]

  &#x20;or court acting on behalf of the ruler.

  [^cite_note-mc13-218]

  &#x20;Tazir punishment is for actions which are considered sinful in Islam, undermine the Muslim community, or threaten public order during Islamic rule, but those that are not punishable as hadd or qisas crimes.

  [^cite_note-234]

  &#x20;The legal restrictions on the exercise of that power are not specified in the Quran or the Hadiths, and vary.

  [^cite_note-msea-235]

  &#x20;The judge enjoys considerable leeway in deciding an appropriate form of punishment, and the punishment does not have to be consistent across the accused persons or over time.

  [^cite_note-mc13-218]

  [^cite_note-msea-235]

  &#x20;The ruler or qadi also has the discretion to forgive tazir offenses.

  [^cite_note-msea-235]

### Socio-political context

The social fabric of pre-modern Islamic societies was largely defined by close-knit communities organized around kinship groups and local neighborhoods. Conflicts between individuals had the potential to escalate into a conflict between their supporting groups and disrupt the life of the entire community. Court litigation was seen as a last resort for cases where informal mediation had failed. This attitude was reflected in the legal maxim "amicable settlement is the best verdict" (*al-sulh sayyid al-ahkam*). In court disputes, qadis were generally less concerned with legal theory than with achieving an outcome that enabled the disputants to resume their previous social relationships. This could be accomplished by avoiding a total loss for the losing side or simply giving them a chance to articulate their position in public and obtain a measure of psychological vindication.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a57–60-236][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009b159–62-237] Islamic law required judges to be familiar with local customs, and they exercised a number of other public functions in the community, including mediation and arbitration, supervision of public works, auditing waqf finances, and looking after the interests of orphans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a11–12-213][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETillier2014-216]



Manuscript copy of al-[Fatawa al-'Alamgiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatawa_al-'Alamgiri?action=edit\&redlink=1)

Unlike pre-modern cultures where the ruling dynasty promulgated the law, Islamic law was formulated by religious scholars without involvement of the rulers. The law derived its authority not from political control, but rather from the collective doctrinal positions of the legal schools (madhhabs) in their capacity as interpreters of the scriptures. The [ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulema) (religious scholars) were involved in management of communal affairs and acted as representatives of the Muslim population vis-à-vis the ruling dynasties, who before the modern era had limited capacity for direct governance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010166–67-238] Military elites relied on the ulema for religious legitimation, with financial support for religious institutions being one of the principal means through which these elites established their legitimacy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerkey2003225–26-239][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010166–67-238] In turn, the ulema depended on the support of the ruling elites for the continuing operation of religious institutions. Although the relationship between secular rulers and religious scholars underwent a number of shifts and transformations in different times and places, this mutual dependence characterized Islamic history until the start of the modern era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHodgson1974176–77-240][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010166–67-238] Additionally, since Sharia contained few provisions in several areas of public law, Muslim rulers were able to legislate various collections of economic, criminal and administrative laws outside the jurisdiction of Islamic jurists, the most famous of which is the *[qanun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanun_\(law\))* promulgated by Ottoman sultans beginning from the 15th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013500-241] The [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) emperor [Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangzeb) (r. 1658–1707) issued a hybrid body of law known as [Fatawa-e-Alamgiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatawa-e-Alamgiri), based on [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) fatwas as well as decisions of Islamic courts, and made it applicable to all religious communities on the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent). This early attempt to turn Islamic law into semi-codified state legislation sparked rebellions against Mughal rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones-Pauly2009-242]

#### Women, non-Muslims, slaves

Main articles: [Women in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam), [Dhimmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi), [Islamic views on slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery), and [History of slavery in the Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_the_Muslim_world)

In both the rules of civil disputes and application of penal law, classical Sharia distinguishes between men and women, between Muslims and non-Muslims, and between free persons and slaves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]



&#x20;[Zanzibar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zanzibar) child [slave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery) sentenced to transport logs by Arab master in Sultanate, 1890s

Traditional Islamic law assumes a patriarchal society with a man at the head of the household.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013502-243] Different legal schools formulated a variety of legal norms which could be manipulated to the advantage of men or women,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidusSalaymeh2014212-244] but women were generally at a disadvantage with respect to the rules of inheritance and witness testimony, where in some cases a woman's witness testimony is effectively treated as half of that of a man.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013502-243] Various financial obligations imposed on the husband acted as a deterrent against [unilateral divorce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_talaq) and commonly gave the wife financial leverage in divorce proceedings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidusSalaymeh2014212-244] Women were active in Sharia courts as both plaintiffs and defendants in a wide variety of cases, though some opted to be represented by a male relative.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidusSalaymeh2014213-245][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

Sharia was intended to regulate affairs of the Muslim community.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Non-Muslims residing under Islamic rule had the legal status of [dhimmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi), which entailed a number of protections, restrictions, freedoms and legal inequalities, including payment of the [jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya) tax.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENettler2009-246] Dhimmi communities had legal autonomy to adjudicate their internal affairs. Cases involving litigants from two different religious groups fell under jurisdiction of Sharia courts,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] where (unlike in secular courts)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEspositoDeLong-Bas201885-247] testimony of non-Muslim witnesses against a Muslim was inadmissible in criminal cases[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasters2009-248] or at all.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014351-249] This legal framework was implemented with varying degree of rigor. In some periods or towns, all inhabitants apparently used the same court without regard for their religious affiliation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] The Mughal emperor Aurangzeb imposed Islamic law on all his subjects, including provisions traditionally applicable only to Muslims, while some of his predecessors and successors are said to have abolished jizya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones-Pauly2009-242][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardy1991566-250] According to Ottoman records, non-Muslim women took their cases to a Sharia court when they expected a more favorable outcome on marital, divorce and property questions than in Christian and Jewish courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidusSalaymeh2014360-251] Over time, non Muslims in the Ottoman Empire could be more or less likely to use Islamic courts. For example, in 1729 at the Islamic court in [Galata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata) only two percent of cases involved non-Muslims whereas in 1789 non-Muslims were a part of thirty percent of cases.**[^cite_note-252]&#x20;**&#x4F;ttoman court records also reflect the use of Islamic courts by formerly non-Muslim women.**[^cite_note-\:2-253]&#x20;**&#x41;s it was illegal for non-Muslims to own Muslims and for non-Muslim men to marry Muslim women in the Ottoman empire, conversion to Islam would have been an option for non-Muslim women to free themselves of a spouse or master they did not want to subject to.**[^cite_note-\:2-253]&#x20;**&#x48;owever, this would likely lead to them being shunned by their former community.[^cite_note-\:2-253]



Chief Eunuch of [Abdul Hamid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_II) (1912)

Classical fiqh acknowledges and regulates slavery as a legitimate institution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013502-243] It granted slaves certain rights and protections, improving their status relative to Greek and Roman law, and restricted the scenarios under which people could be enslaved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis19927-254][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl_Achi2018-255] However, slaves could not inherit or enter into a contract, and were subject to their master's will in a number of ways.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis19927-254][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl_Achi2018-255] The labor and property of slaves were owned by the master, who was also entitled to sexual submission of his unmarried slaves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl_Achi2018-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAli201039-256]

Formal legal disabilities for some groups coexisted with a legal culture that viewed Sharia as a reflection of universal principles of justice, which involved protection of the weak against injustices committed by the strong. This conception was reinforced by the historical practice of Sharia courts, where peasants "almost always" won cases against oppressive landowners, and non-Muslims often prevailed in disputes against Muslims, including such powerful figures as the governor of their province.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009a61-257][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009b167-258] In family matters the Sharia court was seen as a place where the rights of women could be asserted against their husband's transgressions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]

### Under colonial rule

Starting from the 17th century, European powers began to extend political influence over lands ruled by Muslim dynasties, and by the end of the 19th century, much of the Muslim world came under colonial domination. The first areas of Islamic law to be impacted were usually commercial and criminal laws, which impeded colonial administration and were soon replaced by European regulations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010174-259] Islamic commercial laws were also replaced by European (mostly French) laws in Muslim states which retained formal independence, because these states increasingly came to rely on Western capital and could not afford to lose the business of foreign merchants who refused to submit to Islamic regulations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]



[Warren Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Hastings) initiated far-reaching legal reforms in British India.

The first significant changes to the legal system of [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidencies_and_provinces_of_British_India) were initiated in the late 18th century by the governor of Bengal [Warren Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Hastings). Hastings' plan of legal reform envisioned a multi-tiered court system for the Muslim population, with a middle tier of British judges advised by local Islamic jurists, and a lower tier of courts operated by qadis. Hastings also commissioned a translation of the classic manual of Hanafi fiqh, *[Al-Hidayah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hidayah)*, from Arabic into Persian and then English, later complemented by other texts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010176–81-260][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasud2009-261] These translations enabled British judges to pass verdicts in the name of Islamic law based on a combination of Sharia rules and [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) doctrines, and eliminated the need to rely on consultation by local ulema, whom they mistrusted. In the traditional Islamic context, a concise text like *Al-Hidayah* would be used as a basis for classroom commentary by a professor, and the doctrines thus learned would be mediated in court by judicial discretion, consideration of local customs and availability of different legal opinions that could fit the facts of the case. The British use of *Al-Hidayah*, which amounted to an inadvertent codification of Sharia, and its interpretation by judges trained in Western legal traditions anticipated later legal reforms in the Muslim world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010176–81-260][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503-262]

British administrators felt that Sharia rules too often allowed criminals to escape punishment, as exemplified by Hastings' complaint that Islamic law was "founded on the most lenient principles and on an abhorrence of bloodshed".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010176–81-260] In the course of the 19th century, criminal laws and other aspects of the Islamic legal system in India were supplanted by British law, with the exception of Sharia rules retained in family laws and some property transactions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010176–81-260][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasud2009-261] Among other changes, these reforms brought about abolition of slavery, prohibition of child marriage, and a much more frequent use of capital punishment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2009b378-263][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasud2009-261] The resulting legal system, known as *[Anglo-Muhammadan law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Muhammadan_law)*, was treated by the British as a model for legal reforms in their other colonies. Like the British in India, colonial administrations typically sought to obtain precise and authoritative information about indigenous laws, which prompted them to prefer classical Islamic legal texts over local judicial practice. This, together with their conception of Islamic law as a collection of inflexible rules, led to an emphasis on traditionalist forms of Sharia that were not rigorously applied in the pre-colonial period and served as a formative influence on the modern identity politics of the Muslim world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasud2009-261]

### Ottoman Empire



An Ottoman courtroom (1879 AD drawing)

During the colonial era, Muslim rulers concluded that they could not resist European pressure unless they modernized their armies and built centrally administered states along the lines of Western models. In the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), the first such changes in the legal sphere involved placing the formerly independent [waqfs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf) under state control. This reform, passed in 1826, enriched the public treasury at the expense of the waqfs, thereby depleting the financial support for traditional Islamic legal education. Over the second half of the 19th century, a new hierarchical system of secular courts was established to supplement and eventually replace most religious courts. Students hoping to pursue legal careers in the new court system increasingly preferred attending secular schools over the traditional path of legal education with its dimming financial prospects.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010174–76-264]

&#x20;The [Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat) reforms of the 19th century saw reorganization of both Islamic civil law and sultanic criminal law after the model of the [Napoleonic Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] In the 1870s, a codification of civil law and procedure (excepting marriage and divorce), called the *[Mecelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecelle)*, was produced for use in both Sharia and secular courts. It adopted the Turkish language for the benefit of the new legal class who no longer possessed competence in the Arabic idiom of traditional jurisprudence. The code was based on Hanafi law, and its authors selected minority opinions over authoritative ones when they were felt to better "suit the present conditions". The Mecelle was promulgated as a *[qanun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanun_\(law\))* (sultanic code), which represented an unprecedented assertion of the state's authority over Islamic civil law, traditionally the preserve of the ulema.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010174–76-264] The 1917 [Ottoman Law of Family Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_family_law) adopted an innovative approach of drawing rules from minority and majority opinions of all Sunni madhhabs with a modernizing intent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34] The [Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Turkey), which emerged after the [dissolution of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire), abolished its Sharia courts and replaced Ottoman civil laws with the [Swiss Civil Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Civil_Code),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73] but Ottoman civil laws remained in force for several decades in Jordan, Lebanon, Palestine, Syria, and Iraq.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009b-73]



Turkish mufti (17th-century Spanish drawing)

### Nation states

Westernization of legal institutions and expansion of state control in all areas of law, which began during the colonial era, continued in nation-states of the Muslim world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010182–83-265] Sharia courts at first continued to exist alongside state courts as in earlier times, but the doctrine that sultanic courts should implement the ideals of Sharia was gradually replaced by legal norms imported from Europe. Court procedures were also brought in line with European practice. Though the Islamic terms *qadi* and *mahkama* (qadi's/Sharia court) were preserved, they generally came to mean judge and court in the Western sense. While in the traditional Sharia court all parties represented themselves, in modern courts they are represented by professional lawyers educated in Western-style law schools, and the verdicts are subject to review in an appeals court. In the 20th century, most countries abolished a parallel system of Sharia courts and brought all cases under a national civil court system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6]



*Mahkamah Syariyah* (Sharia court) in [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh), Indonesia

In most Muslim-majority countries, traditional rules of classical fiqh have been largely preserved only in family law. In some countries religious minorities such as Christians or Shia Muslims have been subject to separate systems of family laws.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] Many Muslims today believe that contemporary Sharia-based laws are an authentic representation of the pre-modern legal tradition. In reality, they generally represent the result of extensive legal reforms made in the modern era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010182–83-265] As traditional Islamic jurists lost their role as authoritative interpreters of the laws applied in courts, these laws were codified by legislators and administered by state systems which employed a number of devices to effect changes,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] including:

- Selection of alternative opinions from traditional legal literature (*takhayyur*), potentially among multiple madhhabs or denominations, and combining parts of different rulings (*talfiq*).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010182–83-265]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchachtLayish2000155-266]

- Appeal to the classical doctrines of necessity (*darura*), public interest (*maslaha*), and the objectives (*maqasid*) of Sharia, which played a limited role in classical fiqh, but were now given wider utilitarian applications.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010182–83-265]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchachtLayish2000155-266]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503-262]

- Changes in administrative law that grant the courts discretionary powers to restrict certain practices which are not forbidden by substantive law (e.g., polygamy), in some cases imposing penal sanctions as additional deterrence.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010182–83-265]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchachtLayish2000155-266]

- Modernist interpretation of Islamic scriptures without adherence to the rules or methodologies of traditional jurisprudence, known as *neo-ijtihad*.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHallaq2010182–83-265]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchachtLayish2000155-266]



[Muhammad Abduh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abduh) exercised a powerful influence on liberal reformist thought.

The most powerful influence on liberal reformist thought came from the work of the Egyptian Islamic scholar [Muhammad ʿAbduh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abduh) (1849–1905). Abduh viewed only Sharia rules pertaining to religious rituals as inflexible, and argued that the other Islamic laws should be adapted based on changing circumstances in consideration of social well-being. Following precedents of earlier Islamic thinkers, he advocated restoring Islam to its original purity by returning to the Quran and the sunna instead of following the medieval schools of jurisprudence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34] He championed a creative approach to ijtihad that involved direct interpretation of scriptures as well as the methods of *takhayyur* and *talfiq*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34]

One of the most influential figures in modern legal reforms was the Egyptian legal scholar [Abd El-Razzak El-Sanhuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_El-Razzak_El-Sanhuri) (1895–1971), who possessed expertise in both Islamic and Western law. Sanhuri argued that reviving Islamic legal heritage in a way that served the needs of contemporary society required its analysis in light of the modern science of [comparative law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparative_law). He drafted the civil codes of Egypt (1949) and Iraq (1951) based on a variety of sources, including classical fiqh, European laws, existing Arab and Turkish codes, and the history of local court decisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones-Pauly2009-242] Sanhuri's Egyptian code incorporated few classical Sharia rules, but he drew on traditional jurisprudence more frequently for the Iraqi code.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones-Pauly2009-242] Sanhuri's codes were subsequently adopted in some form by most Arab countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34]

Aside from the radical reforms of Islamic family law carried out in Tunisia (1956) and Iran (1967), governments often preferred to make changes that made a clear break from traditional Sharia rules by imposing administrative hurdles rather than changing the rules themselves, in order to minimize objections from religious conservatives. Various procedural changes have been made in a number of countries to restrict polygamy, give women greater rights in divorce, and eliminate child marriage. Inheritance has been the legal domain least susceptible to reform, as legislators have been generally reluctant to tamper with the highly technical system of [Quranic shares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_inheritance_jurisprudence).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones-Pauly2009-242] Some reforms have faced strong conservative opposition. For example, the 1979 reform of Egyptian family law, promulgated by [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat) through presidential decree, provoked an outcry and was annulled in 1985 by the supreme court on procedural grounds, to be later replaced by a compromise version.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34] The [2003 reform of Moroccan family law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudawana), which sought to reconcile universal human rights norms and the country's Islamic heritage, was drafted by a commission that included parliamentarians, religious scholars and feminist activists, and the result has been praised by international rights groups as an example of progressive legislation achieved within an Islamic framework.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERabb2009d-267][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34]

Main article: [Islamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization)

The [Islamic revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival) of the late 20th century brought the topic of Sharia to international attention in the form of numerous political campaigns in the Muslim world calling for full implementation of Sharia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268] A number of factors have contributed to the rise of these movements, classified under the rubric of [Islamism](15012.md) or [political Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Islam), including the failure of authoritarian secular regimes to meet the expectations of their citizens, and a desire of Muslim populations to return to more culturally authentic forms of socio-political organization in the face of a perceived cultural invasion from the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014835-269] Shiite leaders such as [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) drew on leftist anticolonialist rhetoric by framing their call for Sharia as a resistance struggle. They accused secular leaders of corruption and predatory behavior, and claimed that a return to Sharia would replace despotic rulers with pious leaders striving for social and economic justice. In the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) these positions are often encapsulated in the slogan "Islam is the solution" (*al-Islam huwa al-hall*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268]

Full implementation of Sharia theoretically refers to expanding its scope to all fields of law and all areas of public life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] In practice, Islamization campaigns have focused on a few highly visible issues associated with the conservative Muslim identity, particularly women's [hijab](68301.md) and the *hudud* criminal punishments (whipping, stoning and amputation) prescribed for certain crimes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268] For many Islamists, *hudud* punishments are at the core of the divine Sharia because they are specified by the letter of scripture rather than by human interpreters. Modern Islamists have often rejected, at least in theory, the stringent procedural constraints developed by classical jurists to restrict their application.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6] To the broader Muslim public, the calls for Sharia often represent, even more than any specific demands, a vague vision of their current economic and political situation being replaced by a "just utopia".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2014835-269]

A number of legal reforms have been made under the influence of these movements, starting from the 1970s when Egypt and Syria amended their constitutions to specify Sharia as the basis of legislation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268] The [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) of 1979 represented a watershed for Shiism advocates, demonstrating that it was possible to replace a secular regime with a [theocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocracy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268] Several countries, including Iran, Pakistan, Sudan, and some Nigerian states have incorporated hudud rules into their criminal justice systems, which, however, retained fundamental influences of earlier Westernizing reforms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34] In practice, these changes were largely symbolic, and aside from some cases brought to trial to demonstrate that the new rules were being enforced, hudud punishments tended to fall into disuse, sometimes to be revived depending on the local political climate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200820-270] The supreme courts of Sudan and Iran have rarely approved verdicts of stoning or amputation, and the supreme courts of Pakistan and Nigeria have never done so.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200820-270] Nonetheless, Islamization campaigns have also had repercussions in several other areas of law, leading to curtailment of rights of women and religious minorities, and in the case of Sudan contributing to the breakout of a civil war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34]

Advocates of Islamization have often been more concerned with ideology than traditional jurisprudence and there is no agreement among them as to what form a modern Sharia-based "[Islamic state](2646438.md)" should take. This is particularly the case for the theorists of [Islamic economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_economics) and [Islamic finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_finance), who have advocated both free-market and socialist economic models.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayer2009-34] The notion of "Sharia-compliant" finance has become an active area of doctrinal innovation and its development has had a major impact on business operations around the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013503–04-268]

## Contemporary conflicts and applications

Main article: [Application of Sharia by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Application_of_Sharia_by_country)

According to [human rights groups](30335636.md), some of the classical Sharia practices involve serious violations of basic human rights, gender equality and freedom of expression, and the practices of countries governed by Sharia are criticized.[^cite_note-Gontowska,_Luiza_Maria_2005-109] The [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) in Strasbourg (ECtHR) ruled in several cases that Sharia is "incompatible with the fundamental principles of [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy)".[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] "[Human rights concept](13831.md)" have been categorically excluded by the governments of countries such as [Iran](14653.md) and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) under Sharia, claiming that it belongs to [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular) and western values,[^cite_note-Gontowska,_Luiza_Maria_2005-109] while the [Cairo conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Declaration_on_Human_Rights_in_Islam) by the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) declared that human rights can only be respected if they are compatible with Islam.[^cite_note-273]



Use of sharia by country:\
&#x20;

&#x20; Sharia influences family and criminal laws (Libya also added the Sharia).

&#x20; Sharia influences family laws.

&#x20; Sharia plays no role in the judicial system (Sudan declares a secular state in 2020).

&#x20; Regional variations of sharia law apply.

The transformations of Islamic legal institutions in the modern era have had profound implications for the madhhab system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] Legal practice in most of the Muslim world has come to be controlled by government policy and state law, so that the influence of the madhhabs beyond personal ritual practice depends on the status accorded to them within the national legal system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] State law codification commonly utilized the methods of *takhayyur* (selection of rulings without restriction to a particular madhhab) and *talfiq* (combining parts of different rulings on the same question).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] Legal professionals trained in modern law schools have largely replaced traditional [ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulema) as interpreters of the resulting laws.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] Global Islamic movements have at times drawn on different madhhabs and at other times placed greater focus on the scriptural sources rather than classical jurisprudence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] The Hanbali school, with its particularly strict adherence to the Quran and hadith, has inspired conservative currents of direct scriptural interpretation by the [Salafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi) and [Wahhabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabi) movements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201] Other currents, such as networks of Indonesian ulema and Islamic scholars residing in Muslim-minority countries, have advanced [liberal interpretations of Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_and_progressivism_within_Islam) without focusing on traditions of a particular madhhab.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussin2014-201]

### Muslim-majority countries

The legal systems of most Muslim-majority countries can be classified as either secular or mixed. Sharia plays no role in secular legal systems. In mixed legal systems, Sharia rules are allowed to influence some national laws, which are codified and may be based on European or Indian models, and the central legislative role is played by politicians and modern jurists rather than the [ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulema) (traditional Islamic scholars).

Saudi Arabia and some other Persian Gulf states possess what may be called classical Sharia systems, where national law is largely uncodified and formally equated with Sharia, with ulema playing a decisive role in its interpretation. Iran has adopted some features of classical Sharia systems, while also maintaining characteristics of mixed systems, like codified laws and a parliament.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto20088–9-274]

#### Constitutional law

Constitutions of many Muslim-majority countries refer to Sharia as a source or the main source of law, though these references are not in themselves indicative of how much the legal system is influenced by Sharia, and whether the influence has a traditionalist or modernist character.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] The same constitutions usually also refer to universal principles such as democracy and human rights, leaving it up to legislators and the judiciary to work out how these norms are to be reconciled in practice.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200818–19-275] Conversely, some countries (e.g., Algeria), whose constitution does not mention Sharia, possess Sharia-based family laws.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalder2009-7] Nisrine Abiad identifies Bahrain, Iran, Pakistan, and Saudi Arabia as states with "strong constitutional consequences of Sharia "on the organization and functioning of power".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbiad200838–42-276]

#### Family law

Except for secular systems, Muslim-majority countries possess Sharia-based laws dealing with family matters (marriage, inheritance, etc.). These laws generally reflect influence of various modern-era reforms and tend to be characterized by ambiguity, with traditional and modernist interpretations often manifesting themselves in the same country, both in legislation and court decisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200819-33] In some countries (e.g., parts of Nigeria), people can choose whether to pursue a case in a Sharia or secular court.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200819-33][^cite_note-277]

#### Criminal law





Cross amputation survivors, [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983) [Sudan](27421.md); The consequences of accepting the Quran as a source of law and interpreting it literally: "The punishment for those who declared war on God and the Prophet was to have their hands and feet cut off crosswise." ([Al-Ma'idah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ma'idah) 33) A concrete punishment for an abstract crime.

Countries in the Muslim world generally have criminal codes influenced by [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) or [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law), and in some cases a combination of Western legal traditions. Saudi Arabia has never adopted a criminal code and Saudi judges still follow traditional Hanbali jurisprudence. In the course of Islamization campaigns, several countries (Libya, Pakistan, Iran, Sudan, Mauritania, and Yemen) inserted Islamic criminal laws into their penal codes, which were otherwise based on Western models. In some countries only *hudud* penalties were added, while others also enacted provisions for *[qisas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas)* (law of retaliation) and *[diya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\))* (monetary compensation). Iran subsequently issued a new "Islamic Penal Code". The criminal codes of [Afghanistan](737.md) and [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) contain a general provision that certain crimes are to be punished according to Islamic law, without specifying the penalties. Some Nigerian states have also enacted Islamic criminal laws. [Laws in the Indonesian province of Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_criminal_law_in_Aceh) provide for application of discretionary (*[ta'zir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta'zir)*) punishments for violation of Islamic norms, but explicitly exclude *hudud* and *qisas*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETellenbach2015249–50-278] [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei) has been implementing a "Sharia Penal Code", which includes provisions for stoning and amputation, in stages since 2014.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] The countries where *hudud* penalties are legal do not use stoning and amputation routinely, and generally apply other punishments instead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2014-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200820-270][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2017-281]

#### Property law

Sharia recognizes the concept of *haqq*.[^cite_note-282] *Haqq* refers to personal rights of the individual and the right to generate and accumulate wealth. The various ways in which property can be acquired under Sharia are purchase, inheritance, bequest, physical or mental effort, *diya* and donations.[^cite_note-autogenerated139-283] Certain concepts relating to property under Sharia are *Mulk*, *Waqf*, *Mawat* and *Motasarruf*.[^cite_note-autogenerated139-283]

### Court procedures

|  | This section **may contain [original research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:No_original_research)**. Please [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Sharia\&action=edit) by [verifying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability) the claims made and adding [inline citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Citing_sources#Inline_citations). Statements consisting only of original research should be removed. *(March 2019)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |



Shariah court in [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca), Malaysia

Sharia courts traditionally do not rely on lawyers; [plaintiffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaintiffs) and [defendants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defendants) represent themselves. In Saudi Arabia and Qatar, which have preserved traditional procedure in Sharia courts, trials are conducted solely by the judge, and there is no jury system. There is no pre-trial [discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_\(law\)) process, and no [cross-examination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-examination) of witnesses. Unlike common law, judges' verdicts do not set binding [precedents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedents)[^cite_note-284] under the principle of *[stare decisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stare_decisis)*,[^cite_note-285] and unlike civil law, Sharia is left to the interpretation in each case and has no formally [codified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codification_\(law\)) universal statutes.[^cite_note-286]

The [rules of evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rules_of_evidence) in Sharia courts traditionally prioritize oral testimony, and witnesses must be Muslim.[^cite_note-av1-287][^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289][^cite_note-unicef2011-290][^cite_note-mfdl1-291] In criminal cases, women witnesses are unacceptable in stricter, traditional interpretations of Sharia, such as those found in [Hanbali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali) jurisprudence, which forms the basis of law in Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-av1-287]

#### Evidences / testimonies

**In Criminal cases**: A confession, an oath, or the oral testimony of Muslim witnesses are the main evidence admissible in traditional sharia courts for hudud crimes, i.e., the religious crimes of adultery, fornication, rape, accusing someone of illicit sex but failing to prove it, [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam), drinking intoxicants and theft.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto2008663-294][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200831-295]

According to classical jurisprudence, testimony must be from at least two free Muslim male witnesses, or one Muslim male and two Muslim females, who are not related parties and who are of sound mind and reliable character. Testimony to establish the crime of adultery, fornication or rape must be from four Muslim male witnesses, with some [fiqhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh) allowing substitution of up to three male with six female witnesses; however, at least one must be a Muslim male.[^cite_note-296]

[Forensic evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forensic_identification) (*i.e.*, fingerprints, ballistics, blood samples, DNA etc.) and other [circumstantial evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumstantial_evidence) may likewise rejected in [hudud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud) cases in favor of eyewitnesses in some modern interpretations. In the case of regulations that were part of local Malaysian legislation that did not go into effect, this could cause severe difficulties for women plaintiffs in rape cases.[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] In Pakistan, DNA evidence is rejected in paternity cases on the basis of legislation that favors the presumption of children's legitimacy, while in sexual assault cases DNA evidence is regarded as equivalent to expert opinion and evaluated on a case-by-case basis.[^cite_note-299]

**In civil cases:** Quran [2\:282](https://quran.com/2?startingVerse=282) recommends written financial contracts with reliable witnesses, although there is dispute about equality of female testimony.[^cite_note-mfdl1-291]

Marriage is solemnized as a written financial contract, in the presence of two Muslim male witnesses, and it includes a brideprice ([Mahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahr)) payable from a Muslim man to a Muslim woman. The brideprice is considered by a Sharia court as a form of debt. Written contracts were traditionally considered paramount in Sharia courts in the matters of dispute that are debt-related, which includes marriage contracts.[^cite_note-300] Written contracts in debt-related cases, when notarized by a judge, is deemed more reliable.[^cite_note-remes1-301]

In commercial and civil contracts, such as those relating to exchange of merchandise, agreement to supply or purchase goods or property, and others, oral contracts and the testimony of Muslim witnesses historically triumphed over written contracts. Islamic jurists traditionally held that written commercial contracts may be forged.[^cite_note-remes1-301][^cite_note-timku1-302] [Timur Kuran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur_Kuran) states that the treatment of written evidence in religious courts in Islamic regions created an incentive for opaque transactions, and the avoidance of written contracts in economic relations. This led to a continuation of a "largely oral contracting culture" in Muslim-majority nations and communities.[^cite_note-timku1-302][^cite_note-303]

In lieu of written evidence, oaths are traditionally accorded much greater weight; rather than being used simply to guarantee the truth of ensuing testimony, they are themselves used as evidence. Plaintiffs lacking other evidence to support their claims may demand that defendants take an oath swearing their innocence, refusal thereof can result in a verdict for the plaintiff.[^cite_note-304] Taking an oath for Muslims can be a grave act; one study of courts in Morocco found that lying litigants would often "maintain their testimony right up to the moment of oath-taking and then to stop, refuse the oath, and surrender the case."[^cite_note-frank-305] Accordingly, defendants are not routinely required to swear before testifying, which would risk casually profaning the Quran should the defendant commit perjury.[^cite_note-frank-305]

#### Diya

Main article: [Diya (Islam)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\))

In classical jurisprudence monetary compensation for bodily harm (*[diya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diya_\(Islam\))* or blood money) is assessed differently for different classes of victims. For example, for Muslim women the amount was half that assessed for a Muslim man.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307] *Diya* for the death of a free Muslim man is twice as high as for Jewish and Christian victims according to the Maliki and Hanbali madhhabs and three times as high according to Shafi'i rules.[^cite_note-ame1-308] Several legal schools assessed *diya* for [Magians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magians) (*[majus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majus)*) at one-fifteenth the value of a free Muslim male.[^cite_note-ame1-308]

Modern countries which incorporate classical *diya* rules into their legal system treat them in different ways. The Pakistan Penal Code modernized the Hanafi doctrine by eliminating distinctions between Muslims and non-Muslims.[^cite_note-309] In Iran, *diya* for non-Muslim victims professing one of the faiths protected under the constitution (Jews, Christians, and Zoroastrians) was made equal to *diya* for Muslims in 2004,[^cite_note-310] though according to a 2006 US State Department report, the penal code still discriminates against other religious minorities and women.[^cite_note-311] According to Human Rights Watch and the US State Department, in Saudi Arabia Jewish or Christian male plaintiffs are entitled to half the amount a Muslim male would receive, while for all other non-Muslim males the proportion is one-sixteenth.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314]

### Role of fatwas

Main article: [Fatwa](11259.md)

*Fatwa* issued 14 February 1989

> I would like to inform all the intrepid Muslims in the world that the author of the book entitled *The Satanic Verses*, which has been compiled, printed and published in opposition to Islam, the Prophet and the Qur'an, as well as those publishers who were aware of its contents, have been declared *madhur el dam* \[those whose blood must be shed]. I call on all zealous Muslims to execute them quickly, wherever they find them, so that no-one will dare to insult Islam again. Whoever is killed in this path will be regarded as a martyr.[^cite_note-315]

The spread of codified state laws and Western-style legal education in the modern Muslim world has displaced traditional muftis from their historical role of clarifying and elaborating the laws applied in courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHendrickson2013-316][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasudKéchichian2009-317] Instead, fatwas have increasingly served to advise the general public on other aspects of Sharia, particularly questions regarding religious rituals and everyday life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHendrickson2013-316][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMessick2017-318] Modern fatwas deal with topics as diverse as insurance, sex-change operations, moon exploration and beer drinking.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMessick2017-318] Most Muslim-majority states have established national organizations devoted to issuing fatwas, and these organizations to a considerable extent replaced independent muftis as religious guides for the general population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMessickKéchichian2009-319] State-employed muftis generally promote a vision of Islam that is compatible with state law of their country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210]

Modern public and political fatwas have addressed and sometimes sparked controversies in the Muslim world and beyond.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210] [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini)'s [proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rushdie_Affair) condemning [Salman Rushdie](28722.md) to death for his novel *[The Satanic Verses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Satanic_Verses)* is credited with bringing the notion of fatwa to world's attention,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMessick2017-318] although some scholars have argued that it did not qualify as one.[^cite_note-321] Together with later militant fatwas, it has contributed to the popular misconception of the fatwa as a religious death warrant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger2014-322]
