Modern fatwas have been marked by an increased reliance on the process of *[ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)*, i.e. deriving legal rulings based on an independent analysis rather than conformity with the opinions of earlier legal authorities (*[taqlid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqlid)*),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger2014-322] and some of them are issued by individuals who do not possess the qualifications traditionally required of a mufti.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210] The most notorious examples are the fatwas of [militant extremists](8734632.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger2014-322] When [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) and his associates issued a fatwa in 1998 proclaiming "jihad against Jews and Crusaders", many Islamic jurists, in addition to denouncing its content, stressed that bin Laden was not qualified to either issue a fatwa or proclaim a jihad.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210] New forms of ijtihad have also given rise to fatwas that support such notions as gender equality and banking interest, which are at variance with classical jurisprudence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger2014-322]

In the internet age, a large number of websites provide fatwas in response to queries from around the world, in addition to radio shows and satellite television programs offering call-in fatwas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDallalHendrickson2009-210] Erroneous and sometimes bizarre fatwas issued by unqualified or eccentric individuals in recent times have sometimes given rise to complaints about a "chaos" in the modern practice of issuing fatwas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMessick2017-318] There exists no international Islamic authority to settle differences in interpretation of Islamic law. An [International Islamic Fiqh Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Islamic_Fiqh_Academy,_Jeddah) was created by the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md), but its legal opinions are not binding.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasudKéchichian2009-317] The vast amount of fatwas produced in the modern world attests to the importance of Islamic authenticity to many Muslims. However, there is little research available to indicate to what extent Muslims acknowledge the authority of different muftis or heed their rulings in real life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger2014-322]

### Role of *hisba*

Main article: [Hisbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisbah)

Further information: [Islamic religious police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_religious_police)

The classical doctrine of *[hisba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisbah)*, associated with the Quranic injunction of *[enjoining good and forbidding wrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enjoining_good_and_forbidding_wrong)*, refers to the duty of Muslims to promote moral rectitude and intervene when another Muslim is acting wrongly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThielmann2017-323][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMack2018-324] Historically, its legal implementation was entrusted to a public official called *[muhtasib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhtasib)* (market inspector), who was charged with preventing fraud, disturbance of public order and infractions against public morality. This office disappeared in the modern era everywhere in the Muslim world, but it was revived in Arabia by the first Saudi state, and later instituted as a [government committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_the_Promotion_of_Virtue_and_the_Prevention_of_Vice_\(Saudi_Arabia\)) responsible for supervising markets and public order. It has been aided by volunteers enforcing attendance of daily prayers, gender segregation in public places, and a conservative notion of [hijab](68301.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThielmann2017-323] Committee officers were authorized to detain violators before a 2016 reform.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2016-325] With the rising international influence of [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism), the conception of *hisba* as an individual obligation to police religious observance has become more widespread, which led to the appearance of activists around the world who urge fellow Muslims to observe Islamic rituals, dress code, and other aspects of Sharia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThielmann2017-323]



Taliban [religious police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_the_Promotion_of_Virtue_and_the_Prevention_of_Vice_\(Saudi_Arabia\)) beating a woman in [Kabul](16826.md) on 26 August 2001, as reported by [RAWA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Association_of_the_Women_of_Afghanistan),

[^cite_note-326]

[^cite_note-327]

&#x20;for opening her [burqa](165826.md) (face)

In Iran, *hisba* was enshrined in the constitution after the [1979 Revolution](347268.md) as a "universal and reciprocal duty", incumbent upon both the government and the people. Its implementation has been carried out by official committees as well as volunteer forces (*[basij](2892082.md)*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThielmann2017-323][^cite_note-rferl-328] Elsewhere, policing of various interpretations of Sharia-based public morality has been carried out by the [Kano State Hisbah Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_State_Hisbah_Corps) in the Nigerian state of [Kano](2123717.md),[^cite_note-329] by *Wilayatul Hisbah* in the Aceh province of [Indonesia](14579.md),[^cite_note-330] by the [Committee for the Propagation of Virtue and the Prevention of Vice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_the_Propagation_of_Virtue_and_the_Prevention_of_Vice_\(Gaza_Strip\)) in the Gaza Strip, and by the [Taliban](30635.md) during their 1996–2001 and 2021–present rule of Afghanistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThielmann2017-323] Religious police organizations tend to have support from conservative currents of public opinion, but their activities are often disliked by other segments of the population, especially liberals, urban women, and younger people.[^cite_note-331]

In Egypt, a law based on the doctrine of hisba had for a time allowed a Muslim to sue another Muslim over beliefs that may harm society, though because of abuses it has been amended so that only the state prosecutor may bring suit based on private requests.[^cite_note-Gallagher-332] Before the amendment was passed, a hisba suit brought by a group of Islamists against the liberal theologian [Nasr Abu Zayd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasr_Abu_Zayd) on charges of [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam) led to the annulment of his marriage.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334] The law was also invoked in an unsuccessful blasphemy suit against the feminist author [Nawal El Saadawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawal_El_Saadawi).[^cite_note-Gallagher-332] Hisba has also been invoked in several Muslim-majority countries as rationale for blocking pornographic content on the internet and for other forms of faith-based censorship.[^cite_note-335]

### Muslim-minority countries

Sharia also plays a role beyond religious rituals and personal ethics in some countries with Muslim minorities. For example, in Israel Sharia-based family laws are administered for the Muslim population by the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Israel\)) through the Sharia Courts.[^cite_note-336] In India, the Muslim Personal Law (Shariat) Application Act provides for the use of Islamic law for Muslims in several areas, mainly related to family law.[^cite_note-337] In England, the [Muslim Arbitration Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Arbitration_Tribunal) makes use of Sharia family law to settle disputes, though this limited adoption of Sharia is controversial.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]

## Communal support and opposition

### Support

A 2013 survey based on interviews of 38,000 Muslims, randomly selected from urban and rural parts in 39 countries using area probability designs, by the [Pew Forum on Religion and Public Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Forum) found that a majority—in some cases "overwhelming" majority—of Muslims in a number of countries support making "Sharia" or "Islamic law" the [law of the land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_the_land), including Afghanistan (99%), Iraq (91%), Niger (86%), Malaysia (86%), Pakistan (84%), Morocco (83%), Bangladesh (82%), Egypt (74%), Indonesia (72%), Jordan (71%), Uganda (66%), Ethiopia (65%), Mali (63%), Ghana (58%), and Tunisia (56%).[^cite_note-pewssp15-341] In Muslim regions of Southern-Eastern Europe and Central Asia, the support is less than 50%: Russia (42%), Kyrgyzstan (35%), Tajikistan (27%), Kosovo (20%), Albania (12%), Turkey (12%), Kazakhstan (10%), Azerbaijan (8%). Regional averages of support were 84% in South Asia, 77% in Southeast Asia, 74% in the Middle-East/North Africa, 64%, in Sub-Saharan Africa, 18% in Southern-Eastern Europe, and 12% in Central Asia .[^cite_note-pewssp15-341]

However, while most of those who support implementation of Sharia favor using it in family and property disputes, fewer supported application of severe punishments such as whippings and cutting off hands, and interpretations of some aspects differed widely.[^cite_note-pewssp15-341] According to the Pew poll, among Muslims who support making Sharia the law of the land, most do not believe that it should be applied to non-Muslims. In the Muslim-majority countries surveyed this proportion varied between 74% (of 74% in Egypt) and 19% (of 10% in Kazakhstan), as percentage of those who favored making Sharia the law of the land.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStence201348-342]

In all of the countries surveyed, respondents were more likely to define Sharia as "the revealed word of God" rather than as "a body of law developed by men based on the word of God".[^cite_note-343] In analyzing the poll, [Amaney Jamal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amaney_Jamal) has argued that there is no single, shared understanding of the notions "Sharia" and "Islamic law" among the respondents. In particular, in countries where Muslim citizens have little experience with rigid application of Sharia-based state laws, these notions tend to be more associated with Islamic ideals like equality and social justice than with prohibitions.[^cite_note-344] Other polls have indicated that for Egyptians, the word "Sharia" is associated with notions of political, social and gender justice.[^cite_note-345]

In 2008, [Rowan Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowan_Williams), the Archbishop of Canterbury, has suggested that Islamic and [Orthodox Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) courts should be integrated into the British legal system alongside ecclesiastical courts to handle marriage and divorce, subject to agreement of all parties and strict requirements for protection of equal rights for women.[^cite_note-feldman-346] His reference to the Sharia sparked a controversy.[^cite_note-feldman-346] Later that year, [Nicholas Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Phillips,_Baron_Phillips_of_Worth_Matravers), then [Lord Chief Justice of England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Chief_Justice_of_England_and_Wales), stated that there was "no reason why Sharia principles \[...] should not be the basis for mediation or other forms of alternative dispute resolution."[^cite_note-347] A 2008 YouGov poll in the United Kingdom found 40% of Muslim students interviewed supported the introduction of Sharia into British law for Muslims.[^cite_note-348] [Michael Broyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Broyde), professor of law at [Emory University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emory_University) specializing in [alternative dispute resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_dispute_resolution) and [Jewish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_law),[^cite_note-349] has argued that Sharia courts can be integrated into the American religious arbitration system, provided that they adopt appropriate institutional requirements as American rabbinical courts have done.[^cite_note-350]

### Opposition

Further information: [Ban on sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_on_sharia_law)



Protest against Sharia in the United Kingdom (2014)

In the [Western world](21208200.md), Sharia has been called a source of "hysteria",[^cite_note-351] "more controversial than ever", the one aspect of Islam that inspires "particular dread".[^cite_note-kadri-267-8-352] On the Internet, "dozens of self-styled counter-jihadis" emerged to campaign against Sharia law, describing it in strict interpretations resembling those of Salafi Muslims.[^cite_note-kadri-267-8-352] Also, fear of Sharia law and of the ideology of extremism among Muslims as well as certain congregations donating money to terrorist organizations within the Muslim community reportedly spread to mainstream conservative [Republicans](32070.md) in the United States.[^cite_note-TPM-9-23-2010-353] Former [House Speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Newt Gingrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newt_Gingrich) won ovations calling for a federal ban on Sharia law.[^cite_note-TPM-9-23-2010-353] The issue of "liberty versus Sharia" was called a "momentous civilisational debate" by right-wing pundit [Diana West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_West).[^cite_note-354] In 2008 in Britain, the future Prime Minister ([David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron)) declared his opposition to "any expansion of Sharia law in the UK."[^cite_note-BBC-26-3-2008-355] In Germany, in 2014, the Interior Minister ([Thomas de Maizière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_de_Maizi%C3%A8re)) told a newspaper (*[Bild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bild)*), "Sharia law is not tolerated on German soil."[^cite_note-dw-356]

Some countries and jurisdictions have explicit bans on Sharia law. In Canada, for example, sharia law has been explicitly banned in [Quebec](7954867.md) by a 2005 unanimous vote of the National Assembly,[^cite_note-357] while the province of [Ontario](22218.md) allows family law disputes to be arbitrated only under Ontario law.[^cite_note-CHOKSI-14-3-2012-358] In the U.S., opponents of Sharia have sought to ban it from being considered in courts, where it has been routinely used alongside traditional Jewish and Catholic laws to decide legal, business, and family disputes subject to contracts drafted with reference to such laws, as long as they do not violate secular law or the U.S. constitution.[^cite_note-thomas-359] After failing to gather support for a federal law making observing Sharia a felony punishable by up to 20 years in prison, anti-Sharia activists have focused on state legislatures.[^cite_note-thomas-359] By 2014, bills aimed against use of Sharia have been introduced in 34 states and passed in 11.[^cite_note-thomas-359] A notable example of this would be [2010 Oklahoma State Question 755](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Oklahoma_State_Question_755), which sought to permanently ban the use of Sharia law in courts. While approved by voters, the Tenth Circuit Court of Appeals placed an injunction on the law. Citing the unconstitutionality of the law's impartial focus on a specific religion, the law was struck down and never took effect.[^cite_note-360] These bills have generally referred to banning foreign or religious law in order to thwart legal challenges.[^cite_note-thomas-359]

According to Jan Michiel Otto, Professor of Law and Governance in Developing Countries at [Leiden University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leiden_University), "\[a]nthropological research shows that people in local communities often do not distinguish clearly whether and to what extent their norms and practices are based on local tradition, tribal custom, or religion. Those who adhere to a confrontational view of Sharia tend to ascribe many undesirable practices to Sharia and religion overlooking custom and culture, even if high-ranking religious authorities have stated the opposite".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtto200830-361]

## Contemporary theoretical debates

### Compatibility with democracy

It has been argued that the extent to which Sharia is compatible with democracy depends on how it is culturally interpreted,[^cite_note-\:1-362] with a cultural position that Sharia represents the human attempt to interpret God's message associated with a greater preference for democracy than an Islamist interpretation that Sharia law is the literal word of God.[^cite_note-\:1-362]

#### General Muslim views

Scholars [John Esposito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Esposito) and [Natana J. DeLong-Bas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natana_J._DeLong-Bas) distinguish four attitudes toward Sharia and democracy prominent among contemporary Muslims:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEspositoDeLong-Bas2018142–43-363]

- Advocacy of democratic ideas, often accompanied by a belief that they are compatible with Islam, which can play a public role within a democratic system, as exemplified by many protestors who took part in the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) uprisings;

- Support for democratic procedures such as elections, combined with religious or moral objections toward some aspects of Western democracy seen as incompatible with Sharia, as exemplified by Islamic scholars like [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi);

- Rejection of democracy as a Western import and advocacy of traditional Islamic institutions, such as *[shura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shura)* (consultation) and *[ijma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)* (consensus), as exemplified by supporters of absolute monarchy and radical Islamist movements;

- Belief that democracy requires restricting religion to private life: a view held by a minority in the Muslim world.

According to Polls conducted by [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) and [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in [Muslim-majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim-majority) countries, most Muslims see no contradiction between democratic values and religious principles, desiring neither theocracy nor a secular democracy but rather a political model where democratic institutions and values can coexist with the values and principles of Sharia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEspositoDeLong-Bas2018145-364][^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366]

#### Islamic political theories

Main articles: [Political aspects of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_aspects_of_Islam), [Islam and democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_democracy), [Shura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shura), and [Ijma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma)



[Grand Ayatollahs](63899.md) of [Qom](638742.md), Iran; Religious leaders who have the authority to interpret Sharia sources in [Shia Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiism)

[^cite_note-367]

&#x20;used assertive names and titles such as [Ruhollah](26232.md), [Ayatollah](63899.md), [Hujjat al-Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hujjat_al-Islam), which directly connect their identities to Allah or Islam, and gained [tutelage over people and the administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walayah).

[^cite_note-Newman_in_Meri_2006_734-368]

Muslih and Browers identify three major perspectives on democracy among prominent Muslim thinkers who have sought to develop modern, distinctly Islamic theories of socio-political organization conforming to Islamic values and law:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuslihBrowers2009-369]

- The rejectionist Islamic view, elaborated by [Sayyid Qutb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_Qutb) and [Abul A'la Maududi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul_A'la_Maududi), condemns imitation of foreign ideas, distinguishing Western democracy and the Islamic doctrine of *shura* (consultation between ruler and ruled). This perspective, which stresses the comprehensive implementation of Sharia, was widespread in the 1970s and 1980s among various movements seeking to establish an Islamic state, but its popularity has diminished in recent years.

- The moderate Islamic view stresses the concepts of *[maslaha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslaha)* (public interest), *[ʿadl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adl)* (justice), and *shura*. Islamic leaders are considered to uphold justice if they promote public interest, as defined through *shura*. In this view, *shura* provides the basis for representative government institutions that are similar to Western democracy but reflect Islamic rather than Western values. [Hasan al-Turabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_al-Turabi), [Rashid al-Ghannushi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_al-Ghannushi), and [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi) have advocated different forms of this view.

- The liberal Islamic view is influenced by [Muhammad Abduh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abduh)'s emphasis on the role of reason in understanding religion. It stresses democratic principles based on pluralism and freedom of thought. Authors like [Fahmi Huwaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahmi_Huwaidi) and [Tariq al-Bishri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariq_al-Bishri) have constructed Islamic justifications for full citizenship of non-Muslims in an Islamic state by drawing on early Islamic texts. Others, like [Mohammed Arkoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Arkoun) and [Nasr Hamid Abu Zayd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasr_Hamid_Abu_Zayd), have justified pluralism and freedom through non-literalist approaches to textual interpretation. [Abdolkarim Soroush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdolkarim_Soroush) has argued for a "religious democracy" based on religious thought that is democratic, tolerant, and just. Islamic liberals argue for the necessity of constant reexamination of religious understanding, which can only be done in a democratic context.

#### European Court of Human Rights

In 1998, the [Constitutional Court of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Turkey) banned and dissolved Turkey's [Refah Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_Party) over its announced intention to introduce Sharia-based laws, ruling that it would change Turkey's secular order and undermine democracy.[^cite_note-370] On appeal by Refah the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) determined that "sharia is incompatible with the fundamental principles of democracy".[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Refah's Sharia-based notion of a "plurality of legal systems, grounded on religion" was ruled to contravene the [European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights). It was determined that it would "do away with the State's role as the guarantor of individual rights and freedoms" and "infringe the principle of non-discrimination between individuals as regards their enjoyment of public freedoms, which is one of the fundamental principles of democracy".[^cite_note-374] In an analysis, Maurits S. Berger found the ruling to be "nebulous" and surprising from a legal point of view, since the Court neglected to define what it meant by "Sharia" and would not, for example, be expected to regard Sharia rules for Islamic rituals as contravening European human rights values.[^cite_note-375] Kevin Boyle also criticized the decision for not distinguishing between extremist and mainstream interpretations of Islam and implying that peaceful advocacy of Islamic doctrines ("an attitude which fails to respect \[the principle of secularism]") is not protected by the European Convention provisions for freedom of religion.[^cite_note-376]

### Compatibility with freedom of religion

#### Apostasy

Main article: [Apostasy in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam)



Countries that criminalize [religious conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion) as of 2020. Some Muslim-majority countries impose the death penalty or a prison sentence for apostasy away from Islam or ban non-Muslims from [proselytizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytism).

[^cite_note-377]



&#x20;Execution of a [Moroccan Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_Jews) woman ([Sol Hachuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol_Hachuel)) on the grounds of leaving Islam ([apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam)), painting by [Alfred Dehodencq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Dehodencq)

According to Islam, [religious conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion) away from Islam ([apostasy from Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam)) is a sin,[^cite_note-jstor-1570336-378][^cite_note-Lewis-1995-229-379] while "there is no compulsion in religion".[^cite_note-qref|2|256-380] A number of liberal and progressive Islamic scholars have argued that apostasy should not be viewed as a crime.[^cite_note-ELLIOTT-3-26-2006-381][^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-382][^cite_note-jstor-1570336-378][^cite_note-bbcsudan-383] Others argue that the death penalty is an inappropriate punishment,[^cite_note-hassanibrahim-384][^cite_note-smz-385] inconsistent with the Qur'anic verses such as [Al-Baqara 256](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Baqara_256) containing "no compulsion in religion";[^cite_note-ELLIOTT-3-26-2006-381] or that it was a man-made rule enacted in the early Islamic community to prevent and punish the equivalent of desertion or treason.[^cite_note-386] According to [Khaled Abou El Fadl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Abou_El_Fadl), some Muslims do not believe that apostasy requires punishment.[^cite_note-GTWIFE-387] Typically there is a waiting period to allow time to repent and to return to Islam.[^cite_note-jstor-1570336-378][^cite_note-aromar-388][^cite_note-KEY-389][^cite_note-johnesposito-390]

Early Islamic jurists set the standard for apostasy from Islam so high that practically no apostasy verdict could be passed before the 11th century,[^cite_note-Princeton.Enc.2013-391] but later jurists lowered the bar for applying the death penalty, allowing judges to interpret the apostasy law in different ways,[^cite_note-Princeton.Enc.2013-391] which they did sometimes leniently and sometimes strictly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2005291-392] In the late 19th century, criminal penalties for apostasy fell into disuse, although civil penalties were still applied.[^cite_note-jstor-1570336-378] [Wael Hallaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wael_Hallaq) writes that "\[in] a culture whose lynchpin is religion, religious principles and religious morality, apostasy is in some way equivalent to high treason in the modern nation-state".[^cite_note-waelhallaq-393] Some Islamic jurists continue to regard apostasy as a crime deserving the [death penalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_and_corporal_punishment_in_Islam).[^cite_note-aromar-388] Some Sharia interpreters believe apostasy should be enforced only if apostasy becomes a mechanism of [public disobedience and disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitna_\(word\)) (*fitna*).[^cite_note-395]

Twenty-three Muslim-majority countries, as of 2013, penalized apostasy from Islam through their [criminal laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_law).[^cite_note-396] As of 2014, apostasy from Islam was a capital offense in Afghanistan, Brunei, Mauritania, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, Sudan, the United Arab Emirates, and Yemen.[^cite_note-locapo-397][^cite_note-398] In other countries, Sharia courts could use family laws to void the Muslim apostate's [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_Islam) and to deny [child-custody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_custody) rights as well as [inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance) rights.[^cite_note-399] In the years 1985–2006, four individuals were legally executed for apostasy from Islam: "one in Sudan in 1985; two in Iran, in 1989 and 1998; and one in Saudi Arabia in 1992".[^cite_note-ELLIOTT-3-26-2006-381] While modern states have rarely prosecuted apostasy, Islamists have tended to exploit apostasy for political gain.[^cite_note-ELLIOTT-3-26-2006-381] [In a 2008–2012 Pew Research Center poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam#Public_opinion), public support for capital punishment for apostasy among Muslims ranged from 78% in Afghanistan to less than 1% in Kazakhstan, reaching over 50% in 6 of the 20 countries surveyed. Some Sharia courts have limited the freedom of religion for Muslims.[^cite_note-400]

The death penalty[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] or other punishment for apostasy in Islam is a violation of universal [human rights](13831.md), and an issue of [freedom of religion](46006.md) and conscience.[^cite_note-hassanibrahim-384][^cite_note-403]

### Compatibility with human rights

Governments of several predominantly Muslim countries have criticized the [Universal Declaration of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights) (UDHR) for its perceived failure to take into account the cultural and religious context of non-[Western](21208200.md) countries. Iran declared in the UN assembly that UDHR was "a [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) understanding of the [Judeo-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Christian) tradition", which could not be implemented by Muslims without trespassing the Islamic law.[^cite_note-404] Islamic scholars and Islamist political parties consider 'universal human rights' arguments as the imposition of a non-Muslim culture on Muslim people, a disrespect of customary cultural practices and Islam.[^cite_note-as1999-405][^cite_note-406] In 1990, the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md), a group representing all Muslim-majority nations, met in Cairo to respond to the UDHR, then adopted the [Cairo Declaration on Human Rights in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Declaration_on_Human_Rights_in_Islam).[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408]

[Ann Elizabeth Mayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Elizabeth_Mayer) points to notable absences from the Cairo Declaration: provisions for democratic principles, protection for religious freedom, freedom of association, and freedom of the press, as well as equality in rights and equal protection under the law. Article 24 of the Cairo declaration states that "all the rights and freedoms stipulated in this Declaration are subject to the Islamic *shari'a*".[^cite_note-mayer-409]

In 2009, the journal *[Free Inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Inquiry)* summarized the criticism of the Cairo Declaration in an editorial: "We are deeply concerned with the changes to the [Universal Declaration of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights) by a coalition of Islamic states within the [United Nations](31769.md) that wishes to prohibit any criticism of religion and would thus protect Islam's limited view of human rights. In view of the conditions inside the Islamic Republic of Iran, Egypt, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, Sudan, Syria, Bangladesh, Iraq, and Afghanistan, we should expect that at the top of their human rights agenda would be to rectify the legal inequality of women, the suppression of political dissent, the curtailment of free expression, the persecution of ethnic minorities and religious dissenters—in short, protecting their citizens from egregious human rights violations. Instead, they are worrying about protecting Islam".[^cite_note-410]

[H. Patrick Glenn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._Patrick_Glenn) states that Sharia is structured around the concept of mutual obligations of a collective, and it considers individual human rights as potentially disruptive and unnecessary to its revealed code of mutual obligations. In giving priority to this religious collective rather than individual liberty, Islamic law justifies the formal inequality of individuals (women and non-Islamic people).[^cite_note-411] Bassam Tibi states that the Sharia framework and human rights are incompatible.[^cite_note-412] Abdel al-Hakeem Carney, in contrast, states that Sharia is misunderstood from a failure to distinguish *Sharia* from *siyasah* (politics).[^cite_note-413]

#### LGBTQ rights

Main article: [LGBTQ people and Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_people_and_Islam)



Homosexual intercourse illegal:&#x20;

&#x20; [Death penalty](5902.md)

&#x20; Death penalty on books but not applied

&#x20; Up to [life in prison](148681.md)

&#x20; Imprisonment

&#x20; Prison on books but not enforced

Homosexual intercourse is illegal in classical Sharia, with different penalties, including capital punishment, stipulated depending on the situation and legal school. In pre-modern Islam, the penalties prescribed for homosexual acts were "to a large extent theoretical" according to the *[Encyclopaedia of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclopaedia_of_Islam)*, owing in part to stringent procedural requirements for their harsher (*hudud*) forms and in part to prevailing social tolerance toward same-sex relationships.[^cite_note-EI2-414] Historical instances of prosecution for homosexual acts are rare, and those which followed Sharia rules are even rarer.[^cite_note-iranica-law-415] Public attitudes toward homosexuality in the Muslim world turned more negative starting from the 19th century through the [gradual spread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_propagation_of_Salafism_and_Wahhabism) of [Islamic fundamentalist movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism) such as [Salafism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement) and [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism),[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417][^cite_note-dialmy-418] and under the influence of sexual notions prevalent in Europe at that time.[^cite_note-lapidus-419][^cite_note-beckers-420] A number of Muslim-majority[^cite_note-421] countries have retained [criminal penalties for homosexual acts](69713049.md) enacted under colonial rule.[^cite_note-ahmadi-422][^cite_note-economist-423] In recent decades, prejudice against LGBT individuals in the Muslim world has been exacerbated by increasingly conservative attitudes and the rise of Islamist movements, resulting in Sharia-based penalties enacted in several countries.[^cite_note-economist-423] The [death penalty for homosexual acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_penalty_for_homosexuality) is currently a legal punishment in Brunei, Iran, Mauritania, some northern states in Nigeria, Pakistan, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, parts of Somalia, and Yemen, all of which have Sharia-based criminal laws. It is unclear whether the laws of Afghanistan and United Arab Emirates provide for the death penalty for gay sex, *as they have never been carried out*.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-WP-425] Criminalization of consensual homosexual acts and especially making them liable to capital punishment has been condemned by international rights groups. [According to polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_in_Islam#Opinion_polls), the level of social acceptance for homosexuality ranges from 52% among Muslims in the U.S. to less than 10% in a number of Muslim-majority nations.

#### Women

Main articles: [Women in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam) and [Islam and domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_domestic_violence)



&#x20;A *"[cariye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariye)"* or [Ottoman](22278.md) [concubine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concubine), painting by Gustav Richter (1823–1884)

##### Personal status and child marriage

Sharia is the basis for personal status laws in most Islamic-majority nations. These personal status laws determine [rights of women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights) in matters of marriage, divorce, and child custody. A 2011 [UNICEF](19698110.md) report mentions that Sharia law provisions differ for women in financial matters from general human rights provisions. In many countries, in legal proceedings relating to Sharia-based [personal status law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Personal_Status_Law), in financial cases, a [woman's testimony is worth half of a man's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_of_women's_testimony_in_Islam) before a court.[^cite_note-unicef2011-290]

The 1917 codification of Islamic family law in the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) distinguished between the age of competence for marriage, which was set at 18 for boys and 17 for girls, and the minimum age for marriage, which followed the traditional Hanafi limits of 12 for boys and 9 for girls. Marriage below the age of competence was permissible only if proof of sexual maturity was accepted in court, while marriage under the minimum age was forbidden. During the 20th century, most countries in the Middle East followed the Ottoman precedent in defining the age of competence while raising the minimum age to 15 or 16 for boys and 13–16 for girls. Marriage below the age of competence is subject to approval by a judge and the legal guardian of the adolescent. Egypt diverged from this pattern by setting the age limits of 18 for boys and 16 for girls, without a distinction between competence for marriage and minimum age.[^cite_note-EI2-8-29-426]

##### Property rights

Islamic law granted Muslim women certain legal rights, such as property rights which women in the West did not possess until "comparatively recent times".[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428][^cite_note-Feldman-429] Starting with the 20th century, Western legal systems evolved to expand women's rights, but women's rights in the Muslim world have to varying degree remained tied to the Quran, hadiths and their traditional interpretations by Islamic jurists.[^cite_note-alik-430][^cite_note-431] Sharia grants women the [right to inherit property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_property) from other family members, and these rights are detailed in the Quran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorrieChippindale199149-432] A woman's inheritance can be unequal if she inherits from her father, as daughters inherit half as much as their brothers.<sup>\[Quran [4\:11](https://quran.com/4?startingVerse=11)]</sup>[^cite_note-davidpowers-122]

##### Domestic violence

[Jonathan A.C. Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_A.C._Brown) says:

> The vast majority of the ulama across the Sunni schools of law inherited the Prophet's unease over domestic violence and placed further restrictions on the evident meaning of the 'Wife Beating Verse'. A leading Meccan scholar from the second generation of Muslims, [Ata' bin Abi Rabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ata_ibn_Abi_Rabah), counseled a husband not to beat his wife even if she ignored him but rather to express his anger in some other way. [Darimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Darimi), a teacher of both [Tirmidhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirmidhi) and [Muslim bin Hajjaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_ibn_al-Hajjaj) as well as a leading early scholar in Iran, collected all the Hadiths showing Muhammad's disapproval of beating in a chapter entitled 'The Prohibition on Striking Women'. A thirteenth-century scholar from Granada, Ibn Faras, notes that one camp of ulama had staked out a stance forbidding striking a wife altogether, declaring it contrary to the Prophet's example and denying the authenticity of any Hadiths that seemed to permit beating. Even [Ibn Hajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Hajar_al-Asqalani), the pillar of late medieval Sunni Hadith scholarship, concludes that, contrary to what seems to be an explicit command in the Qur'an, the Hadiths of the Prophet leave no doubt that striking one's wife to discipline her actually falls under the Shariah ruling of 'strongly disliked' or 'disliked verging on prohibited'.[^cite_note-433]

The [Surah 4\:34](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An-Nisa,_34), in the Quran, has been debated for domestic violence and also has been subject to [varied interpretations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An-Nisa,_34#Debates_and_discussion_about_the_text).[^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435] According to some interpretations, Sharia condones certain forms of domestic violence against women, when a husband suspects *[nushuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nushuz)* (disobedience, disloyalty, rebellion, ill conduct) in his wife only after admonishing and staying away from the bed does not work.[^cite_note-436] These interpretations have been criticized as inconsistent with women's rights in domestic abuse cases.[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440] [Musawah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musawah), [CEDAW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Discrimination_Against_Women), KAFA and other organizations have proposed ways to modify Sharia-inspired laws to improve women's rights in Muslim-majority nations, including women's rights in domestic abuse cases.[^cite_note-441][^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444]

Others believe that wife-beating is not consistent with a more modernist perspective of the Quran.[^cite_note-445] Many Imams and scholars who learned Shariah in traditional Islamic seminaries object to the misuse of this verse to justify domestic violence. Muslims for [White Ribbon Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Ribbon_Campaign) was launched in 2010 with Imams and Muslim leaders committing to join with others to work to end violence against women.[^cite_note-446] [Khutbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khutbah) campaigns were held in many parts of the world to speak out against domestic violence and encourage Muslim congregants to eradicate domestic abuse.[^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449]

##### Rape

Rape is considered a serious crime in the Sharia law since the Islamic prophet Muhammad ordered rapists to be punished by stoning.[^cite_note-450] The terms *ghasaba* and *ightasaba* have been used by traditional jurists when discussing sexual assault and its punishment. Imam Al-Shāfi'ī defined rape as: "Forcing a woman to commit zinā against her will". To the Ḥanafis, illegal intercourse is considered rape when there is no consent and no deliberate action from the victim. In Mālik's view, rape refers to any kind of unlawful sexual intercourse by usurpation and without consent. This includes instances when the condition of the victims prevents them from expressing their resistance, such as insanity, sleep, or being underage. The [Hanbalites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali), similar to the Mālikites, consider the use of any kind of force as a denial of consent from the victim. The threat of starvation or suffering the cold of winter is also regarded as being against one's will.[^cite_note-Noor-451]

### Slavery

See also: [Islamic views on slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery) and [Islamic views on concubinage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_concubinage)

Sharia authorized the institution of slavery, using the words *abd* (slave) and the phrase *ma malakat aymanukum* ("that which your right hand owns") to refer to women slaves seized as captives of war.[^cite_note-blbr-452][^cite_note-453] Under Islamic law, Muslim men could have sexual relations with female captives and slaves.[^cite_note-454][^cite_note-alik-430][^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456] Sharia, in Islam's history, provided a religious foundation for enslaving non-Muslim women and men but allowed for the [manumission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manumission) of slaves.[^cite_note-457][^cite_note-pl1-458] A slave woman who bore a child to her Muslim master (*umm al-walad*) could not be sold, becoming legally free upon her master's death and the child was considered free and a legitimate heir of the father.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460]

### Terrorism

Further information: [Islamic terrorism](566821.md), [Islamic extremism](8734632.md), [Jihad](16203.md), [Jihadism](7826589.md), and [Salafi jihadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism)



[Al-Qaeda](1921.md) ideologues have used their interpretation of Sharia to justify terrorist attacks.

Some [extremists](8734632.md) have used their interpretation of Islamic scriptures and Sharia, in particular the doctrine of [jihad](16203.md), to justify acts of war and terror against Muslim as well as non-Muslim individuals and governments.[^cite_note-aenexeter-461][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorrieChippindale19914-462][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorrieChippindale1991100-463] The expert on terrorism [Rachel Ehrenfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Ehrenfeld) wrote that the "Sharia's finance ([Islamic banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_banking_and_finance)) is a new weapon in the arsenal of what might be termed [fifth-generation warfare (5GW)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generations_of_warfare#Fifth_generation)".[^cite_note-ZakatForTerror2-464] However, Sharia-compliant financing actually requires a person to stay away from weapons manufacturing.[^cite_note-Jamaldeen-465][^cite_note-JamesChen-466][^cite_note-467]

In classical fiqh, the term *jihad* refers to armed struggle against oppressors.[^cite_note-OEIP-468][^cite_note-469] Classical jurists developed an elaborate set of rules pertaining to jihad, including prohibitions on harming those who are not engaged in combat.[^cite_note-470][^cite_note-471] According to [Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis), "\[a]t no time did the classical jurists offer any approval or legitimacy to what we nowadays call terrorism"[^cite_note-472] and the terrorist practice of suicide bombing "has no justification in terms of Islamic theology, law or tradition".[^cite_note-473] In the modern era the notion of jihad has lost its jurisprudential relevance and instead gave rise to an ideological and political discourse.[^cite_note-hallaq334-474] While modernist Islamic scholars have emphasized defensive and non-military aspects of jihad, some radicals have advanced aggressive interpretations that go beyond the classical theory.[^cite_note-hallaq334-474] For al-Qaeda ideologues, in jihad, all means are legitimate, including targeting Muslim non-combatants and the mass killing of non-Muslim civilians.[^cite_note-aenexeter-461]

Some modern ulema, such as [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi) and [Sulaiman Al-Alwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulaiman_Al-Alwan), have supported attacks against Israeli army reservists and hence should be considered soldiers, while [Hamid bin Abdallah al-Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_bin_Abdallah_al-Ali) declared that suicide attacks in Chechnya were justified as a "sacrifice".[^cite_note-aenexeter-461][^cite_note-475] Many prominent Islamic scholars, including al-Qaradawi himself, have issued condemnations of terrorism in general terms.[^cite_note-476] For example, [Abdul-Aziz ibn Abdullah Al ash-Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Aziz_ibn_Abdullah_Al_ash-Sheikh), the [Grand Mufti of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti_of_Saudi_Arabia) has stated that "terrorizing innocent people \[...] constitute\[s] a form of injustice that cannot be tolerated by Islam", while [Muhammad Sayyid Tantawy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Sayyid_Tantawy), [Grand Imam of al-Azhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Imam_of_al-Azhar) and former [Grand Mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti) of Egypt has stated that "attacking innocent people is not courageous; it is stupid and will be punished on the Day of Judgment".[^cite_note-aenexeter-461][^cite_note-477]

### Blasphemy

Main article: [Islam and blasphemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_blasphemy)



[Blasphemy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_laws) and penalties by jurisdiction:&#x20;

&#x20; Death sentences

&#x20; Prison sentences

&#x20; Fines, other restrictions, or unknown penalty

&#x20; Blasphemy is a common law offence

&#x20; Blasphemy laws repealed

&#x20; No blasphemy laws

In classical fiqh, [blasphemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_blasphemy) refers to any form of cursing, questioning, or annoying God, Muhammad or anything considered sacred in Islam,[^cite_note-khan-478][^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480][^cite_note-481] including denying one of the [Islamic prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_in_Islam) or scriptures, insulting an [angel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angels_in_Islam) or refusing to accept a religious commandment.[^cite_note-482] Jurists of different schools prescribed different punishments for blasphemy against Islam by Muslims and non-Muslims, ranging from imprisonment or fines to the death penalty.[^cite_note-khan-478][^cite_note-483][^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485] In some cases, Sharia allows non-Muslims to escape death by converting and becoming a devout follower of Islam.[^cite_note-486] In the modern [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world), the laws pertaining to blasphemy [vary by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_law), and some countries prescribe punishments consisting of fines, imprisonment, [flogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flogging), [hanging](155019.md), or [beheading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beheading_in_Islam).[^cite_note-psns-487]

Blasphemy laws were rarely enforced in pre-modern Islamic societies, but in the modern era, some states and radical groups have used charges of blasphemy to burnish their religious credentials and gain popular support at the expense of liberal Muslim intellectuals and religious minorities.[^cite_note-488]

Blasphemy, as interpreted under Sharia, is controversial.[^cite_note-489] Representatives of the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) have petitioned the United Nations to condemn "defamation of religions" because "Unrestricted and disrespectful freedom of opinion creates hatred and is contrary to the spirit of peaceful dialogue".[^cite_note-490] The [Cairo Declaration on Human Rights in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Declaration_on_Human_Rights_in_Islam) subjects free speech to unspecified Sharia restrictions: Article 22(a) of the Declaration states that "Everyone shall have the right to express his opinion freely in such manner as would not be contrary to the principles of the Shariah."[^cite_note-491] Others, in contrast, consider blasphemy laws to violate freedom of speech,[^cite_note-492] stating that freedom of expression is essential to empowering both Muslims and non-Muslims, and point to the abuse of blasphemy laws in prosecuting members of religious minorities, political opponents, and settling personal scores.[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494][^cite_note-bbc-blasphemy-495] [In Pakistan, blasphemy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_law_in_Pakistan) have been used to convict more than a thousand people, about half of them [Ahmadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadi) and [Christians](18337522.md).[^cite_note-Böwering-496][^cite_note-bbc-blasphemy-495] While none have been legally executed,[^cite_note-Böwering-496] two Pakistani politicians, [Shahbaz Bhatti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahbaz_Bhatti) and [Salmaan Taseer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmaan_Taseer), have been assassinated over their criticism of the blasphemy laws. The Pakistani blasphemy laws are based upon [colonial-era legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Penal_Code), which made it a "crime to disturb a religious assembly, trespass on burial grounds, insult religious beliefs or intentionally destroy or defile a place or an object of worship", with these laws being modified between 1980 and 1986 by the [military government](1897833.md) of General [Zia-ul Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia-ul_Haq) to make them more severe. A number of clauses were added by the government in order to ["Islamicise" the laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq#'Sharization'_of_Pakistan) and deny the Muslim character of the [Ahmadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadi) minority.[^cite_note-bbc-blasphemy-495]

## Comparison with other legal systems



Legal systems of the world.

[^cite_note-497]

&#x20;Sharia in green, [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) in blue, common law in red.

### Jewish law

Further information: [Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha) and [Seven Laws of Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Laws_of_Noah)

Islamic legal tradition has a number of parallels with [Judaism](15624.md). In both religions, revealed law holds a central place, in contrast to [Christianity](5211.md) which does not possess a body of revealed law, and where theology rather than law is considered to be the principal field of religious study.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlén2003chpt._2a-3][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2013496-498] Both Islamic and Jewish law (*[Halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha)*) are derived from formal textual revelations (Quran and [Pentateuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentateuch)) as well as less formal, orally transmitted prophetic traditions (*[hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith)* and *[mishna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishna)*). According to some scholars, the words *sharia* and *halakha* both mean literally "the path to follow". The *fiqh* literature parallels [rabbinical law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinical_Judaism) developed in the [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud), with fatwas being analogous to [rabbinic *responsa*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_responsa_in_Judaism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlenn2014183–84-499][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMessickKéchichian2009-319]

However, the emphasis on *qiyas* in classical Sunni legal theory is both more explicitly permissive than Talmudic law with respect to authorizing individual reason as a source of law, and more implicitly restrictive, in excluding other, unauthorized forms of reasoning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlenn2014183–84-499]

### Common law systems

Main article: [Common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law)

Early Islamic law developed a number of legal concepts that anticipated similar such concepts that later appeared in English [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law).[^cite_note-Makdisi-500][^cite_note-Devichand-501] Similarities exist between the royal English [contract](19280537.md) protected by the action of [debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt) and the Islamic *Aqd*, between the English [assize of novel disseisin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assize_of_novel_disseisin) and the Islamic *Istihqaq*, and between the English [jury](44720.md) and the Islamic *Lafif* in classical Maliki jurisprudence.[^cite_note-Makdisi-500][^cite_note-502] The [law schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_school) known as [Inns of Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inns_of_Court) also parallel [Madrasahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasah).[^cite_note-Makdisi-500] The methodology of legal [precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedent) and reasoning by [analogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analogy) (*[Qiyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas)*) are also similar in both the Islamic and common law systems,[^cite_note-503] as are the English [trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trust_law) and [agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agency_\(law\)) institutions to the Islamic *[Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)* and *[Hawala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawala)* institutions, respectively.[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505][^cite_note-Devichand-501]

Elements of Islamic law also have other parallels in Western legal systems. For example, the influence of Islam on the development of an international law of the sea can be discerned alongside that of the Roman influence.[^cite_note-Tai-506] [George Makdisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Makdisi) has argued that the madrasa system of attestation paralleled the legal scholastic system in the West, which gave rise to the modern university system. The triple status of *[faqih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faqih)* ("[master of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_of_Laws)"), *[mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti)* ("professor of [legal opinions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatw%C4%81)") and *mudarris* ("teacher"), conferred by the classical Islamic legal degree, had its equivalents in the medieval Latin terms *[magister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magister_\(degree\))*, *[professor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professor)* and *[doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_\(title\))*, respectively, although they all came to be used synonymously in both East and West.[^cite_note-G-Makdisi-507]

Makdisi suggested that the medieval European doctorate, *licentia docendi* was modeled on the Islamic degree *ijazat al-tadris wa-l-ifta*, of which it is a word-for-word translation, with the term *ifta'* (issuing of fatwas) omitted.[^cite_note-G-Makdisi-507][^cite_note-508] He also argued that these systems shared fundamental freedoms: the freedom of a professor to profess his personal opinion and the freedom of a student to pass judgement on what he is learning.[^cite_note-G-Makdisi-507]

There are differences between Islamic and Western legal systems. For example, Sharia classically recognizes only [natural persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_persons), and never developed the concept of a [legal person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_person), or [corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporation), i.e., a legal entity that [limits the liabilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited_liability) of its managers, shareholders, and employees; exists beyond the lifetimes of its founders; and that can own assets, sign contracts, and appear in court through representatives.[^cite_note-509] Interest prohibitions imposed secondary costs by discouraging record keeping and delaying the introduction of modern accounting.[^cite_note-510] Such factors, according to Timur Kuran, have played a significant role in retarding economic development in the Middle East.[^cite_note-511] However, the rise of monopoly wealth and corporations have proven to also be detrimental to the economic equality of a society. Ziauddin Sardar also suggests that the promotion of equitable wealth distribution and suppression of monopoly capital are a part of Islam's message that emphasises genuine equity and justice.[^cite_note-512]

[^cite_note-13]: &#x20;[Twelver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver) Shia jurisprudence does not recognize the use of qiyas, but relies on reason (*ʿaql*) in their place.<sup>[\[11\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlén2003chpt._4c-11)</sup><sup>[\[12\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneider2014-12)</sup>

[^cite_note-63]: &#x20;"...the essential features of old Muhammadan jurisprudence, such as the idea of the 'living tradition' of the ancient schools of law \[local practices of early Muslim communities]; a body of common doctrine expressing the earliest effort to systematize; legal maxims which often reflect a slightly later stage, and an important nucleus of legal traditions... it is safe to say that \[this] Muhammadan legal science started in the later part of the Umaiyad period, taking the legal practice of the time as its raw material and endorsing, modifying, or rejecting it"<sup>[\[61\]](#cite_note-Schacht-OoMJ-1959-190-62)</sup>

[^cite_note-65]: &#x20;Islamic "law did not derive directly from the Koran but developed... out of popular and administrative practices under the Umayyads, and this practice often diverged from the intentions and even the explicit wording of the Koran... Norms derived from the Koran were introduced into Muhammadan law almost invariably at a secondary stage"<sup>[\[62\]](#cite_note-128-schacht-64)</sup>

[^cite_note-68]: &#x20;"In the time of Shafi'i, traditions from the Prophet were already recognized as one of the material bases of Muhammadan law. Their position in the ancient schools of law was, as we have seen, much less certain."<sup>[\[63\]](#cite_note-Schacht-OoMJ-1959-40-66)</sup> Another example is that an early major works of fiqh—[Muwatta Imam Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muwatta_Imam_Malik) (edited by Shaibani)—contains 429 ahadith by Muhammad but 750 by the [Companions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahabah), [Successors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabi'un) and others,<sup>[\[64\]](#cite_note-Schacht-OoMJ-1959-22-67)</sup> in contrast to later works by al-Bukhari, Muslim, etc. that contain only ahadith by Muhammad.

[^cite_note-70]: &#x20;"...a great many traditions in the classical and other collections were put into circulation only after Shafi'i's time; the first considerable body of legal traditions from the Prophet originated towards the middle of the second century..."<sup>[\[65\]](#cite_note-Schacht-OoMJ-1959-4-69)</sup>

[^cite_note-78]: &#x20;Sharia is the first of [Four Doors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Doors) and the lowest level on the path to [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam) in [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) and in branches of Islam that are influenced by Sufism, such as [Ismailism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailism) and [Alawism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alawism). It is necessary to reach from Sharia to [Tariqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariqa), from there to [Ma'rifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'rifa) and finally to [haqiqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haqiqa). In each of these gates, there are 10 levels that the [dervish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dervish) must pass through.<sup>[\[72\]](#cite_note-Sevim-77)</sup>

[^cite_note-86]: &#x20;"What theology is for the Christian, law is for the Muslim."<sup>[\[78\]](#cite_note-Smith-1957-57-84)</sup> referenced in<sup>[\[79\]](#cite_note-Forte-1978-2-85)</sup>

[^cite_note-118]: &#x20;According to some researchers like [John Wansbrough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wansbrough) and [Yehuda D. Nevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehuda_D._Nevo),<sup>[\[109\]](#cite_note-116)</sup> later known as the [Revisionist school of Islamic studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revisionist_school_of_Islamic_studies), the canonization of the Quran, through the unification of "various previous texts" into a single, unchanging standard text and the elimination of others, took place over a period of approximately 200 years. They argued that a common method within Islamic tradition was to legitimize a practice by attributing it to a past authority that enjoyed high popular acceptance. This suggests that the accounts of Uthman's cancellation and burning of the codices may actually refer to a single issue encompassing different codices.<sup>[\[110\]](#cite_note-117)</sup> While variant readings were initially scorned and criticized as human creations, five centuries later these readings were sanctified by being linked to a divine source.<sup>[\[85\]](#cite_note-auto-92)</sup>

[^cite_note-140]: &#x20;"The full systems of Islamic theology and law are not derived primarily from the Quran. Muhammad's sunna was a second but far more detailed living scripture, and later Muslim scholars would thus often refer to Muhammad as 'The Possessor of Two Revelations'".<sup>[\[131\]](#cite_note-JACBMM2014\:18-139)</sup>

[^cite_note-181]: &#x20;By 1980, veiling was required in government and educational settings in [Iran](14653.md), with the 1983 penal code imposing 74 lashes for not adhering to the hijab, though the exact requirements were unclear.<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-Ramezani10-177)</sup><sup>[\[166\]](#cite_note-bucar-175)</sup><sup>[\[169\]](#cite_note-178)</sup> This led to public tensions and vigilante actions regarding proper hijab.<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-Ramezani10-177)</sup><sup>[\[166\]](#cite_note-bucar-175)</sup> Subsequent regulations in 1984 and 1988 clarified dress-code standards, and the current penal code prescribes fines or prison terms for failing to observe hijab, without detailing its specific form.<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-Ramezani10-177)</sup><sup>[\[170\]](#cite_note-kelly-breslin-179)</sup><sup>[\[171\]](#cite_note-180)</sup>

[^cite_note-184]: &#x20;[Beyza Bilgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyza_Bilgin) states that the expression 'let them put their outer coverings over themselves' in the 59th verse of [Al-Ahzab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-A%E1%B8%A5z%C4%81b) was revealed because they harassed women under the conditions of that day, considering them to be concubines, and commented as follows:<sup>[\[173\]](#cite_note-\:0-183)</sup>"In other words, veiling is a security issue that arose according to the needs of that period. These are not taken into consideration at all and are reflected as God's command. Women have been called God's command for a thousand years. Women said the same thing to their daughters and daughters-in-law."She said the following about covering herself in [prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah) :"They tell me; 'Do you cover yourself while praying?' Of course, I cover up when I'm in congregation. I am obliged not to disturb the peace. But I also pray with my head uncovered in my own home. Because the Quran's requirement for prayer is not covering up, but ablution and turning towards the qibla. This is a thousand year old issue. It's so ingrained in us. But this should definitely not be underestimated. Because people do it thinking it is God's command. But on the other hand, we should not declare a person who does not cover up as a bad woma&#x6E;*.*"<sup>[\[173\]](#cite_note-\:0-183)</sup>

[^cite_note-191]: &#x20; In Shiite jurisprudence, it is unlawful for a master of a female slave to grant a third party the use of her for sexual relations. The Shiite scholar [Shaykh al-Tusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaykh_al-Tusi) stated: ولا يجوز إعارتها للاستمتاع بها لأن البضع لا يستباح بالإعارة "It is not permissible to loan (the slave girl) for enjoyment purpose, because sexual intercourse cannot be legitimate through loaning"<sup>[\[178\]](#cite_note-189)</sup> and the Shiite scholars al-Muhaqiq al-Kurki, [Allamah Al-Hilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allamah_Al-Hilli) and Ali Asghar Merwarid made the following ruling: ولا تجوز استعارة الجواري للاستمتاع "It is not permissible to loan the slave girl for the purpose of sexual intercourse"<sup>[\[179\]](#cite_note-190)</sup>

[^cite_note-204]: &#x20;The Ibadis reject the belief that the leader of the Muslim community must be descended from the [Quraysh tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quraysh_tribe). (This differs from the Sunni belief and also the Shia belief which holds that ideally and eventually Muslims will be ruled by the [Mahdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi), who will be descended from Muhammad's household – *[Ahl al-Bayt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Bayt)* – Muhammad having been a member of the Quraysh tribe.)<sup>[\[190\]](#cite_note-diana27-202)</sup><sup>[\[191\]](#cite_note-haw200-203)</sup>

[^cite_note-321]: &#x20;Khomeini himself did not call this proclamation a fatwa, and in Islamic legal theory only a court can decide whether an accused is guilty. However, after the proclamation was presented as a fatwa in Western press, this characterization was widely accepted by both its critics and its supporters.<sup>[\[303\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHendrickson2013-316)</sup><sup>[\[307\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVikør2005142-320)</sup>

[^cite_note-395]: &#x20;According to the interpretation of [Turkish Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Muslims) [scholar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_studies) Ahmet Albayrak regarding apostasy from Islam as a form of wrongdoing, punishment for leaving Islam is not a sign of [intolerance towards other religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_intolerance), and it is not aimed at one's freedom to leave Islam and [to choose a different faith](46006.md). In his opinion, it is more correct to say that the punishment is imposed as a safety precaution when conditions warrant the imposition of it; for example, the punishment is imposed if apostasy from Islam becomes a mechanism of [public disobedience and disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitna_\(word\)) (*fitna*).<sup>[\[380\]](#cite_note-394)</sup>

[ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [1478-1913](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1478-1913). In classical Muslim doctrine on war, likewise, genuine non-combatants are not to be harmed. These include women, minors, servants and slaves who do not take part in the fighting, the blind, monks, hermits, the aged, those physically unable to fight, the insane, the delirious, farmers who do not fight, traders, merchants, and contractors. The main criterion distinguishing combatants from non-combatants is that the latter do not fight and do not contribute to the war effort.
